```text
64	
65	\section{NVE Threshold Sensitivity Analysis}
66	\label{sec:appendix_sensitivity}
67	
68	We report the full sensitivity analysis for the NVE diagnostic framework introduced in Section~\ref{sec:results}. The main text reports Task~B2 (any CIVO detection, $\nve \geq 1.5\times$). Here we additionally present Task~B1, which restricts the positive class to \emph{sustained} CIVO, specifically experiments exhibiting persistent, accelerating value overestimation that progresses to performance collapse. Transient CIVO cases (e.g., exp~009b, which self-corrects after a brief excursion above the threshold) are classified as negative in B1.
69	
70	\subsection{Per-Experiment NVE Values}
71	
72	Table~\ref{tab:nve_raw} lists the NVE values at 35K+ training steps for all eight factorial experiments. Experiments are sorted by descending NVE. Experiment 008e\_v3\_s2 exhibits elevated NVE ($3.78\times$) due to Imagination-Reality Divergence (IRD), a mechanistically distinct phenomenon from CIVO, and is classified as negative in both tasks.
73	
74	\begin{table}[h]
75	\centering
76	\caption{Per-experiment NVE values (35K+ steps) and ground truth labels. $+$ = positive (CIVO), $-$ = negative. IRD = Imagination-Reality Divergence; trans.\ = transient CIVO with self-correction.}
77	\label{tab:nve_raw}
78	\small
79	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrccc@{}}
80	\toprule
81	Experiment & NVE & Mechanism & B1 & B2 \\
82	\midrule
83	006          & 4.84 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\
84	008d         & 4.13 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\
85	008e\_v3\_s2 & 3.78 & IRD (non-CIVO) & $-$ & $-$ \\
86	009b         & 1.62 & transient CIVO & $-$ & $+$ \\
87	001          & 1.00 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
88	008f         & 0.97 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
89	008e\_v3\_s1 & 0.94 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
90	008a         & 0.93 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
91	\bottomrule
92	\end{tabular}
93	\end{table}
94	
95	\subsection{Multi-Threshold Classification Performance}
96	
97	Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1} reports Task~B1 classification metrics across six NVE thresholds. Task~B1 achieves $\text{AUROC} = 1.000$ because the minimum positive NVE ($4.13\times$, exp~008d) exceeds the maximum negative NVE ($3.78\times$, exp~008e\_v3\_s2), yielding perfect rank separation. The optimal threshold lies in the interval $(3.78, 4.13]$; we report $\geq 3.9\times$ as a representative value. Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2} provides the corresponding analysis for Task~B2 ($\text{AUROC} = 0.933$, imperfect separation due to 008e\_v3\_s2).
98	
99	\begin{table*}[t]
100	\centering
101	\caption{\textbf{Task~B1}: Sustained CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\text{pos}}=2$, $n_{\text{neg}}=6$, $n=8$, $\text{AUROC}=1.000$). All confidence intervals are two-sided Clopper-Pearson exact binomial ($\alpha=0.05$). Thresholds 1.2$\times$/1.5$\times$ and 1.7$\times$/2.0$\times$ yield identical operating points (no NVE values in those intervals). $^\dagger$Optimal threshold in $(3.78, 4.13]$.}
102	\label{tab:sensitivity_b1}

115	\rowcolor{gray!15}

The single false positive at $\geq 1.5\times$ is 008e\_v3\_s2 (IRD mechanism, not CIVO). The $1.5\times$ threshold (highlighted) maximizes F1 while maintaining perfect recall.}

141	
142	\subsection{Threshold Recommendation}
143	
144	For the main diagnostic (Task~B2), we recommend $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ as the operating threshold for three reasons:
145	
146	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
147	\item \textbf{Maximum recall.} The $1.5\times$ threshold achieves 100\% recall, detecting all three CIVO cases including the transient case (009b, $\nve = 1.62\times$). Raising the threshold to $1.7\times$ loses 009b, dropping recall to 66.7\%.
148	\item \textbf{Best F1.} At $1.5\times$, F1 = 85.7\%, the highest among all tested thresholds for B2. The single false positive (008e\_v3\_s2) is mechanistically identifiable as IRD rather than CIVO.
149	\item \textbf{Sensitivity--specificity tradeoff.} Lower thresholds ($1.0\times$) add false positives without gaining true positives. Higher thresholds ($\geq 1.7\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\_v3\_s2 false positive (which requires $\tau > 3.78$).
150	\end{enumerate}
151	
152	For Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), perfect classification is achievable at $\nve \geq 3.9\times$, but with only $n_{\text{pos}} = 2$ the statistical power is severely limited (accuracy 95\% CI: [63.1\%, 100\%]).
153	
154	\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 8$ experiments, all Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals are wide (e.g., 87.5\% accuracy has 95\% CI [47.3\%, 99.7\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds.
155	
156	% --- APPENDIX B: Factorial Ablation Details ---
157	
158	\section{Factorial Ablation Details}
159	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
160	
161	This appendix provides the full factorial ablation results summarized in \S\ref{sec:f3f5_summary} of the main text.
162	
163	\subsection{F3: Unexposed Temporal Embedding Suppresses Value Drift}
164	\label{sec:f3}
165	
166	\begin{figure}[t]
167	\centering
168	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_factorial_grid.pdf}
169	\caption{Factorial isolation of CIVO trigger. Only the combination of trained $e_{\dt}$ and multi-rate data (upper-left cell) produces pathological NVE. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}
170	\label{fig:factorial_grid}
171	\end{figure}
172	
173	The causal claim that trained embedding \emph{plus} multi-rate exposure jointly trigger CIVO rests on two complementary controls that each remove one factor while holding the other constant.
174	
175	\paragraph{Evidence A: exposure is necessary (exp\_008a).}
176	Exp\_008a uses a trained embedding ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) that is never exposed to $\dt{>}1$ transitions during world-model training (subsample OFF). Despite sharing the same embedding architecture as exp\_008d, its NVE remains in the $0.59$--$0.93\times$ range across 105K steps. Performance follows two V-shaped recovery cycles, reaching 777.7 at 105K (85\% of baseline). The critical trigger is not the embedding's capacity to represent temporal information, but its adaptation to multi-rate data that enables precise but inaccurate $\dt{>}1$ state predictions within the RSSM.
177	
178	This contrasts with exp\_006 (trained $e_{\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}15$), where NVE accelerates from $1.61\times$ at 45K to $2.31\times$ at 55K before catastrophic collapse to 132.9 at 65K. Both experiments use identical trained embeddings ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) and $H{=}15$, differing only in whether the world model sees $\dt{>}1$ transitions during training, confirming that multi-rate data exposure, not embedding architecture, is the operative variable.
179	
180	\paragraph{Evidence B: trainability is necessary (exp\_015b).}
181	Exp\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \texttt{requires\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.55\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \rightarrow 1.26 \rightarrow 1.45 \rightarrow 0.65 \rightarrow 1.05 \rightarrow 0.60 \rightarrow 0.59 \rightarrow 0.55$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \S\ref{sec:pathways}), indicating that the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.
182	
183	The contrast with exp\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \rightarrow 1.71 \rightarrow 2.09 \rightarrow 4.12$) and triggers full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\times$ before self-correcting to $0.55\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\dt$-embedding must be able to \emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.
184	
185	Together, these two controls establish that CIVO requires the \emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.
186	
187	Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run replicates the causal pattern: trained $e_{\dt}$ triggers CIVO (exp\_010b, NVE $8.27\times$, collapse to 51.7) while removing the embedding eliminates it (exp\_024b, 2 seeds, NVE $<0.2\times$, eval 96.5/77.3). This extends the necessary-condition finding from Walker Walk to a second locomotion environment with qualitatively different dynamics (6-joint vs.\ 2-joint).
188	
189	\begin{table}[h]
190	\centering
191	\small
192	\caption{Causal isolation: $e_{\dt}$ configuration $\times$ multi-rate data exposure. The combination of trained embedding and multi-rate exposure is the sole trigger for CIVO. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}
193	\label{tab:causal_matrix}
194	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
195	\toprule
196	& Multi-rate data (\textsc{on}) & Single-rate data (\textsc{off}) \\
197	\midrule
198	Trained $e_{\dt}$ & exp\_008d: \textbf{CIVO} & exp\_008a: healthy \\
199	 & NVE 4.12$\times$, collapse & NVE 0.93$\times$ \\
200	\midrule
201	Frozen $e_{\dt}$ & exp\_015b: grad.\ starvation & --- \\
202	 & peak NVE 1.45$\times$ (transient) & \\
203	\midrule
204	Removed $e_{\dt}$ & exp\_008f: no CIVO & --- \\
205	 & NVE $\leq$0.97$\times$ & \\
206	\bottomrule
207	\end{tabular}
208	\end{table}
209	
210	Only the upper-left cell (trained embedding with multi-rate exposure) triggers CIVO (Table~\ref{tab:causal_matrix}). This 2$\times$2 structure establishes that both factors are jointly necessary.
211	
212	\subsection{F4: Degradation Scales Monotonically with \texorpdfstring{$\dt$}{dt}}
213	\label{sec:f4}
214	
215	\begin{figure}[t]
216	\centering
217	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig5_dt_amplification.pdf}
218	\caption{Peak evaluation return as a function of imagination $\dt$. Performance degrades monotonically: $\dt{=}1$ (915.7) $>$ $\dt{=}2$ (485.3, $-47\%$) $>$ $\dt{=}4$ (176.9, $-81\%$).}
219	\label{fig:dt_amplification}
220	\end{figure}
221	
222	The magnitude of degradation scales monotonically with the imagination step size (Figure~\ref{fig:dt_amplification}). From the $\dt{=}1$ baseline (915.7 at 105K), fixed $\dt{=}2$ (exp\_006) achieves a peak of only 485.3 ($-47\%$), and fixed $\dt{=}4$ (exp\_007) peaks at 176.9 ($-81\%$). This monotonic relationship is consistent with compounding model error: larger temporal gaps amplify the RSSM's prediction inaccuracy at each imagination step, and these errors accumulate across the rollout horizon.
223	
224	\subsection{F5: Imagination Horizon and Subsampling Are Secondary Factors}
225	\label{sec:f5}
226	
227	Neither horizon length nor temporal subsampling triggers collapse without multi-rate data exposure: both trained-but-unexposed configurations (008a, 008c) maintain $\nve < 0.93\times$ (\S\ref{sec:f3}), while the trained-and-exposed counterpart (008d) collapses with $\nve = 4.12\times$.  The no-embedding configurations show cross-seed variance (008f: 503.0 vs.\ 009c: 148.3) but neither exhibits $\nve > 1.5\times$, confirming that low evaluation alone does not indicate CIVO.
228	
229	The $3.2\times$ performance gap (008a: 777.7 vs.\ 008c: 240.9) quantifies the horizon effect independently of CIVO, as both conditions maintain healthy NVE ($\leq$0.93$\times$).
230	
231	% --- APPENDIX C: Full Analysis & Discussion ---
232	
233	\section{Full Analysis \& Discussion}
234	\label{sec:appendix_analysis}
235	
236	This appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.
237	
238	\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}
239	\label{sec:civo_mechanism}
240	
241	The RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO.  The trained $\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.
242	
243	This asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\nve$ above $1.5\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.
244	
245	A random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\times$ higher mean reward than $\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\approx 36\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\approx 64\%$ to actor exploitation, confirming that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.
246	
247	This mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced: the reward head's co-adaptation with the temporal embedding produces systematically upward-biased predictions at $\dt > 1$.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.
248	
249	\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping and Gradient Decomposition}
250	\label{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}
251	
252	\subsubsection{Gradient Decomposition Results}
253	\label{sec:gradient_decomposition}
254	
255	\begin{figure}[t]
256	\centering
257	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_gradient_decomposition.pdf}
258	\caption{Gradient contribution to $e_{\Delta t}$ by loss component at two training checkpoints. The reward head contributes the largest share (38--49\%) yet reward-only SGS (exp\_016e) fails; the observation decoder channel (16--22\%) carries the critical $\dt$-specific spatial information that drives the RSSM fidelity gap.}
259	\label{fig:gradient_decomp}
260	\end{figure}
261	
262	Exp\_016e collapses in the same qualitative pattern as the unmodified control: peak evaluation 326.7 at 75K steps followed by catastrophic decline to 31.7 at 95K, with NVE reaching $10.42\times$. The SGS intervention neither prevented nor attenuated CIVO.
263	
264	To explain this null result, we performed a gradient decomposition analysis using \texttt{torch.autograd.grad} to measure each loss component's contribution to $\nabla_\theta e_{\Delta t}$, where $\theta$ denotes the temporal embedding parameters. Table~\ref{tab:gradient_decomposition} reports the results.
265	
266	\begin{table}[h]
267	\centering
268	\caption{Gradient contributions to the temporal embedding $e_{\Delta t}$ by loss component, measured via \texttt{torch.autograd.grad} with training configuration \texttt{gate\_fixed\_dt=2}. Gradient norms are $\ell_2$ norms of $\nabla_\theta \mathcal{L}_i$ for each loss $\mathcal{L}_i$.}
269	\label{tab:gradient_decomposition}
270	\small
271	\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
272	\toprule
273	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{@55K}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{@112K}} \\
274	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5}
275	Loss component & Share & (\%) & Share & (\%) \\
276	\midrule
277	Reward head & --- & 49.0 & --- & 38.1 \\
278	KL dynamics & --- & 34.7 & --- & 40.4 \\
279	Observation decoders & --- & 16.3 & --- & 21.5 \\
280	\bottomrule
281	\end{tabular}
282	\end{table}
283	
284	The gradient decomposition reveals a counterintuitive structure: the reward head provides the largest gradient contribution (49.0\% at 55K, 38.1\% at 112K), yet reward-only SGS (exp\_016e) fails catastrophically (NVE $10.42\times$). The observation decoder channel contributes the smallest share (16.3\% at 55K, 21.5\% at 112K) yet carries the critical $\dt$-specific spatial information.  This dissociation between gradient magnitude and causal importance explains why exp\_016e, which targets the largest pathway, is functionally insufficient for CIVO prevention.
285	
286	The gradient structure is relatively stable across training (reward shifts from 49\% to 38\%, obs from 16\% to 22\%, KL from 35\% to 40\%), unlike what a magnitude-based account would predict. The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects the scalar reward prediction task's sensitivity to temporal embedding parameters, but this gradient does not encode $\Delta t$-specific structural information about spatial dynamics. The observation decoder channel, despite its smaller magnitude, is the only pathway that encodes how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales.
287	
288	\paragraph{Why the smallest channel is causally critical.} The observation decoder is the only loss component whose gradients encode how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states ($14$ orientations, $9$ velocities, $1$ height) across temporal scales.  This $\dt$-specific spatial information shapes the latent state representation in ways that propagate to reward prediction: the RSSM fidelity gap ($2.78\times$ reward MAE ratio, Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) arises because the temporal embedding learns to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\dt{>}1$, not because the reward head directly learns scale-dependent biases.  Full-SGS targets this causal chain by preventing observation-decoder gradients from updating $e_{\Delta t}$, though its 50\% recovery rate (1/2 seeds) indicates that breaking the gradient pathway alone does not fully eliminate the vulnerability.
289	
290	\subsubsection{Implications for the CIVO Mechanism}
291	\label{sec:sgs_implications}
292	
293	The gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude and causal importance are decoupled in CIVO.
294	
295	\paragraph{Gradient importance $\neq$ gradient magnitude.} The reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\%) to $e_{\Delta t}$, yet reward-only SGS fails.  The observation decoder contributes the smallest share (16--22\%), yet its removal (via Full-SGS) is necessary for CIVO mitigation.  This dissociation arises because the reward head's gradient, while large, does not encode $\dt$-specific structural information: it adjusts $e_{\Delta t}$ to minimize a scalar prediction error that is largely $\dt$-invariant.  The observation decoder's gradient, though smaller, encodes how the RSSM should differentially reconstruct 24-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales. This is the channel through which $e_{\Delta t}$ learns scale-dependent latent biases.
296	
297	\paragraph{Reframing the intervention target.} Any gradient-level strategy that targets the reward head alone (reward prediction regularization, reward-conditioned value clipping, or reward-only SGS) targets the largest pathway but not the informationally critical one.  The failure of exp\_016e (NVE $10.42\times$) despite detaching the dominant gradient source confirms this.  Full-pathway SGS (exp\_018) detaches $e_{\Delta t}$ from all non-KL gradients, retaining only the KL dynamics contribution (34--40\%).  However, this intervention yields mixed results: seed~1 avoids collapse (eval 448.3) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8), indicating that removing the observation decoder gradient channel is necessary but not sufficient.
298	
299	Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (exp\_021) further clarifies the role of Full-SGS.  The intervention did not prevent the initial CIVO onset: NVE peaked at $9.94\times$ at 45K (comparable to the unmitigated exp\_010b's $8.27\times$).  However, unlike exp\_010b, which collapsed permanently to eval 51.7, exp\_021 self-corrected within ${\sim}$10K steps, with sustained NVE dropping to $0.34\times$ (55K--105K) and final evaluation recovering to 221.5.  This pattern reframes Full-SGS as \emph{enabling recovery from CIVO} rather than preventing its onset: the gradient detachment constrains value growth after the transient spike, allowing the critic to recalibrate.  Performance nonetheless remains at 38.7\% of baseline (572.5), likely because Full-SGS over-aggressively blocks actor gradients (actor gradient norms near zero throughout training).  Combined with exp\_018's mixed Walker results (1/2 seeds), these findings indicate that gradient-level interventions alone do not fully eliminate the CIVO vulnerability, and that additional mechanisms (e.g., forward-pass conditioning effects, residual capacity-dependent instabilities) operate beyond the gradient pathway.
300	
301	\paragraph{Connection to RSSM fidelity analysis.} The trained-embedding condition (exp\_008d) shows a $2.78\times$ reward gap between $\Delta t{=}1$ and $\Delta t{=}2$ transitions (Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}).  This gap arises because the observation decoder gradients shape $e_{\Delta t}$ to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\dt{>}1$, and the reward head inherits these biases \textit{through the latent state} rather than learning them directly.  The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects sensitivity to $e_{\Delta t}$'s parameters but not causal responsibility for the fidelity gap.
302	
303	\subsubsection{Entropy Dynamics Across Configurations}
304	\label{sec:entropy_dynamics}
305	
306	\begin{figure}[h]
307	\centering
308	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_entropy_dynamics.pdf}
309	\caption{Actor entropy trajectories for three configurations. All start at ${\sim}8$ nats and converge to similar values (${\sim}{-}4.5$ to ${-}5$). The CIVO-triggering configuration (008d, red) follows a normal entropy reduction trajectory; the divergence from the control (008f) is subtle in entropy space, consistent with CIVO operating through the value channel rather than the entropy channel. 018v2 (Full-SGS, seed~1) shows slightly lower terminal entropy.}
310	\label{fig:entropy_dynamics}
311	\end{figure}
312	
313	\subsubsection{Exp\_016e Reinterpreted as Stochastic Variation}
314	\label{sec:016e_reinterp}
315	
316	The gradient decomposition establishes that exp\_016e targets the largest gradient pathway (reward: 38--49\%) but not the informationally critical one (observation decoders: 16--22\%).  The apparent timing differences between exp\_016e and exp\_008d (later collapse onset, 75K vs.\ 25K, and higher peak evaluation, 326.7 vs.\ 167.2) must be attributed to stochastic variation rather than SGS effects, since the critical observation decoder channel remains intact.  All three trajectories (exp\_008d, exp\_009b, exp\_016e) share the invariant identified by the NVE diagnostic: each crosses the $1.5\times$ pathological threshold at some point during training.
317	
318	\subsection{Post-CIVO Outcomes: Why Seeds Diverge}
319	\label{sec:seed_divergence}
320	
321	\begin{table}[h]
322	\centering
323	\caption{Post-CIVO outcomes for the 008d configuration. Peak/Final NVE measured in the 35K+ window.}
324	\label{tab:post_civo}
325	\footnotesize
326	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
327	\begin{tabular}{lcccl}
328	\toprule
329	\textbf{Seed} & \textbf{Peak} & \textbf{Final} & \textbf{Eval} & \textbf{Outcome} \\
330	\midrule
331	s1 (008d) & 4.12$\times$ & 4.12$\times$ & 82.8 & Full collapse \\
332	s2 (009b) & 1.62$\times$ & 0.38$\times$ & 267.9 & Transient CIVO \\
333	\bottomrule
334	\end{tabular}
335	\end{table}
336	
337	The two post-CIVO outcomes (Table~\ref{tab:post_civo}), full collapse and transient CIVO with self-correction, both cross the $\nve > 1.5\times$ threshold, yet the downstream damage is stochastic.\footnote{Exp\_009e was excluded from multi-seed analyses due to a seed configuration error (default seed=0, identical to exp\_008d).}  Bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10000$) over the $n{=}2$ effective seeds yields peak NVE mean $3.085$ (95\% CI: $[2.05, 4.12]$), collapse rate $0.5$, and Cohen's $d = 2.60$ (mean difference $2.205$) relative to the no-embedding control, confirming a large effect despite the limited sample.  We hypothesize that the divergence relates to the critic's representational capacity at the time of CIVO onset.  \citet{lyle2022capacity} demonstrate that deep RL networks suffer progressive capacity loss, in which early-learned features become increasingly difficult to overwrite.  If the critic's capacity is already partially consumed when CIVO injects overestimation, the network cannot recalibrate (seed~0); if sufficient capacity remains, the critic can absorb transient overestimation and recover (seed~2).
338	
339	\subsection{Why MPC Is Immune}
340	\label{sec:mpc_immune}
341	
342	\citet{nhu2025tawm} show that $\dt$-conditioning benefits TD-MPC2, raising the question of why it fails in DreamerV3.  The critical difference is that MPC re-plans from real states at every step, so imagination errors do not accumulate into the training signal because there is no critic bootstrapping from imagined trajectories.  In DreamerV3, the actor-critic trains entirely within imagination, and any systematic reward-prediction bias at $\dt > 1$ is amplified through the closed actor-critic loop.
343	
344	\subsection{The \texorpdfstring{$\dt$}{dt}-Embedding Dilemma}
345	\label{sec:embedding_dilemma}
346	
347	The factorial results reveal a fundamental tension: removing the $\dt$-embedding (008f) eliminates CIVO but at a $13\times$ cost to reward prediction quality ($0.7469$ vs.\ $0.0568$ MAE), as the world model cannot distinguish temporal scales.  Restricting imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding (008e\_v3) avoids CIVO in 1 of 2 seeds but the other collapses via Pathway~B (\S\ref{sec:f6}).  Neither approach alone provides a complete solution.
348	
349	\subsection{Multiple Vulnerability Pathways}
350	\label{sec:pathways}
351	
352	The two collapse mechanisms identified in \S\ref{sec:f6}, CIVO (Pathway~A, value-driven, entropy decreasing) and non-CIVO instability (Pathway~B, eval-driven, entropy increasing), have distinct diagnostic signatures but a shared root cause: the trained $\dt$-embedding's learned features affect both reward prediction across temporal scales (Pathway~A) and the representation space in which the actor operates (Pathway~B).  This explains why $\nve$ is sensitive but not perfectly specific: Pathway~B's late evaluation decline inflates $\nve$ through the same arithmetic as CIVO, yielding the sole false positive ($3.78\times$ for 008e\_v3 s2).  Distinguishing the two requires examining the temporal profile and entropy direction of collapse.
353	
354	\subsection{Implications for Temporal Abstraction in World Models}
355	\label{sec:implications}
356	
357	CIVO identifies a structural incompatibility between flat temporal conditioning and imagination-based actor-critic training.  Hierarchical approaches (THICK \citep{gumbsch2024thick}, Hieros \citep{mattes2024hieros}, LS-Imagine \citep{li2025lsimagine}) may avoid this by maintaining separate dynamics per temporal scale.  Whether CIVO transfers to transformer-based world models (DreamerV4; \citealt{hafner2025dreamerv4}) depends on whether attention weights co-adapt with temporal embeddings analogously.
358	
```

</details>

The main text reports Task~B2 (any CIVO detection, $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$). Here we additionally present Task~B1, which restricts the positive class to \\emph{sustained} CIVO, specifically experiments exhibiting persistent, accelerating value overestimation that progresses to performance collapse. Transient CIVO cases (e.g., exp~009b, which self-corrects after a brief excursion above the threshold) are classified as negative in B1.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Experiment NVE Values}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:nve_raw} lists the NVE values at 35K+ training steps for all eight factorial experiments. Experiments are sorted by descending NVE. Experiment 008e\\_v3\\_s2 exhibits elevated NVE ($3.78\\times$) due to Imagination-Reality Divergence (IRD), a mechanistically distinct phenomenon from CIVO, and is classified as negative in both tasks.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-experiment NVE values (35K+ steps) and ground truth labels. $+$ = positive (CIVO), $-$ = negative. IRD = Imagination-Reality Divergence; trans.\\ = transient CIVO with self-correction.}\n\\label{tab:nve_raw}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nExperiment & NVE & Mechanism & B1 & B2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n006          & 4.84 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\\\\n008d         & 4.13 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\\\\n008e\\_v3\\_s2 & 3.78 & IRD (non-CIVO) & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n009b         & 1.62 & transient CIVO & $-$ & $+$ \\\\\n001          & 1.00 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008f         & 0.97 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008e\\_v3\\_s1 & 0.94 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n008a         & 0.93 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Threshold Classification Performance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1} reports Task~B1 classification metrics across six NVE thresholds. Task~B1 achieves $\\text{AUROC} = 1.000$ because the minimum positive NVE ($4.13\\times$, exp~008d) exceeds the maximum negative NVE ($3.78\\times$, exp~008e\\_v3\\_s2), yielding perfect rank separation. The optimal threshold lies in the interval $(3.78, 4.13]$; we report $\\geq 3.9\\times$ as a representative value. Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2} provides the corresponding analysis for Task~B2 ($\\text{AUROC} = 0.933$, imperfect separation due to 008e\\_v3\\_s2).\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{\\textbf{Task~B1}: Sustained CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\\text{pos}}=2$, $n_{\\text{neg}}=6$, $n=8$, $\\text{AUROC}=1.000$). All confidence intervals are two-sided Clopper-Pearson exact binomial ($\\alpha=0.05$). Thresholds 1.2$\\times$/1.5$\\times$ and 1.7$\\times$/2.0$\\times$ yield identical operating points (no NVE values in those intervals). $^\\dagger$Optimal threshold in $(3.78, 4.13]$.}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_b1}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nThreshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\geq 1.0\\times$ & 2 & 3 & 0 & 3 & 62.5\\% \\; [24.5, 91.5] & 40.0\\% & 100\\% & 57.1\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.2\\times$ & 2 & 2 & 0 & 4 & 75.0\\% \\; [34.9, 96.8] & 50.0\\% & 100\\% & 66.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.5\\times$ & 2 & 2 & 0 & 4 & 75.0\\% \\; [34.9, 96.8] & 50.0\\% & 100\\% & 66.7\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.7\\times$ & 2 & 1 & 0 & 5 & 87.5\\% \\; [47.3, 99.7] & 66.7\\% & 100\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 2.0\\times$ & 2 & 1 & 0 & 5 & 87.5\\% \\; [47.3, 99.7] & 66.7\\% & 100\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 3.9\\times^\\dagger$ & 2 & 0 & 0 & 6 & 100\\% \\; [63.1, 100] & 100\\% & 100\\% & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{\\textbf{Task~B2}: Any CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\\text{pos}}=3$, $n_{\\text{neg}}=5$, $n=8$, $\\text{AUROC}=0.933$). The single false positive at $\\geq 1.5\\times$ is 008e\\_v3\\_s2 (IRD mechanism, not CIVO). The $1.5\\times$ threshold (highlighted) maximizes F1 while maintaining perfect recall.}\n\\label{tab:sensitivity_b2}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nThreshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\geq 1.0\\times$ & 3 & 2 & 0 & 3 & 75.0\\% \\; [34.9, 96.8] & 60.0\\% & 100\\% & 75.0\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.2\\times$ & 3 & 1 & 0 & 4 & 87.5\\% \\; [47.3, 99.7] & 75.0\\% & 100\\% & 85.7\\% \\\\\n\\rowcolor{gray!15}\n$\\geq 1.5\\times$ & 3 & 1 & 0 & 4 & 87.5\\% \\; [47.3, 99.7] & 75.0\\% & 100\\% & 85.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 1.7\\times$ & 2 & 1 & 1 & 4 & 75.0\\% \\; [34.9, 96.8] & 66.7\\% & 66.7\\% & 66.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 2.0\\times$ & 2 & 1 & 1 & 4 & 75.0\\% \\; [34.9, 96.8] & 66.7\\% & 66.7\\% & 66.7\\% \\\\\n$\\geq 3.9\\times$ & 2 & 0 & 1 & 5 & 87.5\\% \\; [47.3, 99.7] & 100\\% & 66.7\\% & 80.0\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Threshold Recommendation}\n\nFor the main diagnostic (Task~B2), we recommend $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ as the operating threshold for three reasons:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Maximum recall.} The $1.5\\times$ threshold achieves 100\\% recall, detecting all three CIVO cases including the transient case (009b, $\\nve = 1.62\\times$). Raising the threshold to $1.7\\times$ loses 009b, dropping recall to 66.7\\%.\n\\item \\textbf{Best F1.} At $1.5\\times$, F1 = 85.7\\%, the highest among all tested thresholds for B2. The single false positive (008e\\_v3\\_s2) is mechanistically identifiable as IRD rather than CIVO.\n\\item \\textbf{Sensitivity--specificity tradeoff.} Lower thresholds ($1.0\\times$) add false positives without gaining true positives. Higher thresholds ($\\geq 1.7\\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\\_v3\\_s2 false positive (which requires $\\tau > 3.78$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nFor Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), perfect classification is achievable at $\\nve \\geq 3.9\\times$, but with only $n_{\\text{pos}} = 2$ the statistical power is severely limited (accuracy 95\\% CI: [63.1\\%, 100\\%]).\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 8$ experiments, all Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals are wide (e.g., 87.5\\% accuracy has 95\\% CI [47.3\\%, 99.7\\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds.\n\n% --- APPENDIX B: Factorial Ablation Details ---\n\n\\section{Factorial Ablation Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nThis appendix provides the full factorial ablation results summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:f3f5_summary} of the main text.\n\n\\subsection{F3: Unexposed Temporal Embedding Suppresses Value Drift}\n\\label{sec:f3}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_factorial_grid.pdf}\n\\caption{Factorial isolation of CIVO trigger. Only the combination of trained $e_{\\dt}$ and multi-rate data (upper-left cell) produces pathological NVE. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{fig:factorial_grid}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe causal claim that trained embedding \\emph{plus} multi-rate exposure jointly trigger CIVO rests on two complementary controls that each remove one factor while holding the other constant.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence A: exposure is necessary (exp\\_008a).}\nExp\\_008a uses a trained embedding ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) that is never exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during world-model training (subsample OFF). Despite sharing the same embedding architecture as exp\\_008d, its NVE remains in the $0.59$--$0.93\\times$ range across 105K steps. Performance follows two V-shaped recovery cycles, reaching 777.7 at 105K (85\\% of baseline). The critical trigger is not the embedding's capacity to represent temporal information, but its adaptation to multi-rate data that enables precise but inaccurate $\\dt{>}1$ state predictions within the RSSM.\n\nThis contrasts with exp\\_006 (trained $e_{\\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}15$), where NVE accelerates from $1.61\\times$ at 45K to $2.31\\times$ at 55K before catastrophic collapse to 132.9 at 65K. Both experiments use identical trained embeddings ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) and $H{=}15$, differing only in whether the world model sees $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during training, confirming that multi-rate data exposure, not embedding architecture, is the operative variable.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence B: trainability is necessary (exp\\_015b).}\nExp\\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.55\\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \\rightarrow 1.26 \\rightarrow 1.45 \\rightarrow 0.65 \\rightarrow 1.05 \\rightarrow 0.60 \\rightarrow 0.59 \\rightarrow 0.55$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \\S\\ref{sec:pathways}), indicating that the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.\n\nThe contrast with exp\\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \\rightarrow 1.71 \\rightarrow 2.09 \\rightarrow 4.12$) and triggers full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\\times$ before self-correcting to $0.55\\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\\dt$-embedding must be able to \\emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.\n\nTogether, these two controls establish that CIVO requires the \\emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run replicates the causal pattern: trained $e_{\\dt}$ triggers CIVO (exp\\_010b, NVE $8.27\\times$, collapse to 51.7) while removing the embedding eliminates it (exp\\_024b, 2 seeds, NVE $<0.2\\times$, eval 96.5/77.3). This extends the necessary-condition finding from Walker Walk to a second locomotion environment with qualitatively different dynamics (6-joint vs.\\ 2-joint).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Causal isolation: $e_{\\dt}$ configuration $\\times$ multi-rate data exposure. The combination of trained embedding and multi-rate exposure is the sole trigger for CIVO. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{tab:causal_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n& Multi-rate data (\\textsc{on}) & Single-rate data (\\textsc{off}) \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008d: \\textbf{CIVO} & exp\\_008a: healthy \\\\\n & NVE 4.12$\\times$, collapse & NVE 0.93$\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFrozen $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_015b: grad.\\ starvation & --- \\\\\n & peak NVE 1.45$\\times$ (transient) & \\\\\n\\midrule\nRemoved $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008f: no CIVO & --- \\\\\n & NVE $\\leq$0.97$\\times$ & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOnly the upper-left cell (trained embedding with multi-rate exposure) triggers CIVO (Table~\\ref{tab:causal_matrix}). This 2$\\times$2 structure establishes that both factors are jointly necessary.\n\n\\subsection{F4: Degradation Scales Monotonically with \\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}}\n\\label{sec:f4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig5_dt_amplification.pdf}\n\\caption{Peak evaluation return as a function of imagination $\\dt$. Performance degrades monotonically: $\\dt{=}1$ (915.7) $>$ $\\dt{=}2$ (485.3, $-47\\%$) $>$ $\\dt{=}4$ (176.9, $-81\\%$).}\n\\label{fig:dt_amplification}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe magnitude of degradation scales monotonically with the imagination step size (Figure~\\ref{fig:dt_amplification}). From the $\\dt{=}1$ baseline (915.7 at 105K), fixed $\\dt{=}2$ (exp\\_006) achieves a peak of only 485.3 ($-47\\%$), and fixed $\\dt{=}4$ (exp\\_007) peaks at 176.9 ($-81\\%$). This monotonic relationship is consistent with compounding model error: larger temporal gaps amplify the RSSM's prediction inaccuracy at each imagination step, and these errors accumulate across the rollout horizon.\n\n\\subsection{F5: Imagination Horizon and Subsampling Are Secondary Factors}\n\\label{sec:f5}\n\nNeither horizon length nor temporal subsampling triggers collapse without multi-rate data exposure: both trained-but-unexposed configurations (008a, 008c) maintain $\\nve < 0.93\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:f3}), while the trained-and-exposed counterpart (008d) collapses with $\\nve = 4.12\\times$.  The no-embedding configurations show cross-seed variance (008f: 503.0 vs.\\ 009c: 148.3) but neither exhibits $\\nve > 1.5\\times$, confirming that low evaluation alone does not indicate CIVO.\n\nThe $3.2\\times$ performance gap (008a: 777.7 vs.\\ 008c: 240.9) quantifies the horizon effect independently of CIVO, as both conditions maintain healthy NVE ($\\leq$0.93$\\times$).\n\n% --- APPENDIX C: Full Analysis & Discussion ---\n\n\\section{Full Analysis \\& Discussion}\n\\label{sec:appendix_analysis}\n\nThis appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.\n\n\\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:civo_mechanism}\n\nThe RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO.  The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.\n\nThis asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\\nve$ above $1.5\\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.\n\nA random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \\textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\\times$ higher mean reward than $\\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \\textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\\approx 36\\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\\approx 64\\%$ to actor exploitation, confirming that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.\n\nThis mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \\citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced: the reward head's co-adaptation with the temporal embedding produces systematically upward-biased predictions at $\\dt > 1$.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.\n\n\\subsection{Selective Gradient Stopping and Gradient Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}\n\n\\subsubsection{Gradient Decomposition Results}\n\\label{sec:gradient_decomposition}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_gradient_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient contribution to $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component at two training checkpoints. The reward head contributes the largest share (38--49\\%) yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails; the observation decoder channel (16--22\\%) carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information that drives the RSSM fidelity gap.}\n\\label{fig:gradient_decomp}\n\\end{figure}\n\nExp\\_016e collapses in the same qualitative pattern as the unmodified control: peak evaluation 326.7 at 75K steps followed by catastrophic decline to 31.7 at 95K, with NVE reaching $10.42\\times$. The SGS intervention neither prevented nor attenuated CIVO.\n\nTo explain this null result, we performed a gradient decomposition analysis using \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} to measure each loss component's contribution to $\\nabla_\\theta e_{\\Delta t}$, where $\\theta$ denotes the temporal embedding parameters. Table~\\ref{tab:gradient_decomposition} reports the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Gradient contributions to the temporal embedding $e_{\\Delta t}$ by loss component, measured via \\texttt{torch.autograd.grad} with training configuration \\texttt{gate\\_fixed\\_dt=2}. Gradient norms are $\\ell_2$ norms of $\\nabla_\\theta \\mathcal{L}_i$ for each loss $\\mathcal{L}_i$.}\n\\label{tab:gradient_decomposition}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@55K}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{@112K}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5}\nLoss component & Share & (\\%) & Share & (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nReward head & --- & 49.0 & --- & 38.1 \\\\\nKL dynamics & --- & 34.7 & --- & 40.4 \\\\\nObservation decoders & --- & 16.3 & --- & 21.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals a counterintuitive structure: the reward head provides the largest gradient contribution (49.0\\% at 55K, 38.1\\% at 112K), yet reward-only SGS (exp\\_016e) fails catastrophically (NVE $10.42\\times$). The observation decoder channel contributes the smallest share (16.3\\% at 55K, 21.5\\% at 112K) yet carries the critical $\\dt$-specific spatial information.  This dissociation between gradient magnitude and causal importance explains why exp\\_016e, which targets the largest pathway, is functionally insufficient for CIVO prevention.\n\nThe gradient structure is relatively stable across training (reward shifts from 49\\% to 38\\%, obs from 16\\% to 22\\%, KL from 35\\% to 40\\%), unlike what a magnitude-based account would predict. The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects the scalar reward prediction task's sensitivity to temporal embedding parameters, but this gradient does not encode $\\Delta t$-specific structural information about spatial dynamics. The observation decoder channel, despite its smaller magnitude, is the only pathway that encodes how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales.\n\n\\paragraph{Why the smallest channel is causally critical.} The observation decoder is the only loss component whose gradients encode how the RSSM should differentially predict multi-dimensional proprioceptive states ($14$ orientations, $9$ velocities, $1$ height) across temporal scales.  This $\\dt$-specific spatial information shapes the latent state representation in ways that propagate to reward prediction: the RSSM fidelity gap ($2.78\\times$ reward MAE ratio, Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) arises because the temporal embedding learns to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, not because the reward head directly learns scale-dependent biases.  Full-SGS targets this causal chain by preventing observation-decoder gradients from updating $e_{\\Delta t}$, though its 50\\% recovery rate (1/2 seeds) indicates that breaking the gradient pathway alone does not fully eliminate the vulnerability.\n\n\\subsubsection{Implications for the CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:sgs_implications}\n\nThe gradient decomposition reveals that gradient magnitude and causal importance are decoupled in CIVO.\n\n\\paragraph{Gradient importance $\\neq$ gradient magnitude.} The reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\\%) to $e_{\\Delta t}$, yet reward-only SGS fails.  The observation decoder contributes the smallest share (16--22\\%), yet its removal (via Full-SGS) is necessary for CIVO mitigation.  This dissociation arises because the reward head's gradient, while large, does not encode $\\dt$-specific structural information: it adjusts $e_{\\Delta t}$ to minimize a scalar prediction error that is largely $\\dt$-invariant.  The observation decoder's gradient, though smaller, encodes how the RSSM should differentially reconstruct 24-dimensional proprioceptive states across temporal scales. This is the channel through which $e_{\\Delta t}$ learns scale-dependent latent biases.\n\n\\paragraph{Reframing the intervention target.} Any gradient-level strategy that targets the reward head alone (reward prediction regularization, reward-conditioned value clipping, or reward-only SGS) targets the largest pathway but not the informationally critical one.  The failure of exp\\_016e (NVE $10.42\\times$) despite detaching the dominant gradient source confirms this.  Full-pathway SGS (exp\\_018) detaches $e_{\\Delta t}$ from all non-KL gradients, retaining only the KL dynamics contribution (34--40\\%).  However, this intervention yields mixed results: seed~1 avoids collapse (eval 448.3) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8), indicating that removing the observation decoder gradient channel is necessary but not sufficient.\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (exp\\_021) further clarifies the role of Full-SGS.  The intervention did not prevent the initial CIVO onset: NVE peaked at $9.94\\times$ at 45K (comparable to the unmitigated exp\\_010b's $8.27\\times$).  However, unlike exp\\_010b, which collapsed permanently to eval 51.7, exp\\_021 self-corrected within ${\\sim}$10K steps, with sustained NVE dropping to $0.34\\times$ (55K--105K) and final evaluation recovering to 221.5.  This pattern reframes Full-SGS as \\emph{enabling recovery from CIVO} rather than preventing its onset: the gradient detachment constrains value growth after the transient spike, allowing the critic to recalibrate.  Performance nonetheless remains at 38.7\\% of baseline (572.5), likely because Full-SGS over-aggressively blocks actor gradients (actor gradient norms near zero throughout training).  Combined with exp\\_018's mixed Walker results (1/2 seeds), these findings indicate that gradient-level interventions alone do not fully eliminate the CIVO vulnerability, and that additional mechanisms (e.g., forward-pass conditioning effects, residual capacity-dependent instabilities) operate beyond the gradient pathway.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to RSSM fidelity analysis.} The trained-embedding condition (exp\\_008d) shows a $2.78\\times$ reward gap between $\\Delta t{=}1$ and $\\Delta t{=}2$ transitions (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}).  This gap arises because the observation decoder gradients shape $e_{\\Delta t}$ to produce latent states that are spatially biased at $\\dt{>}1$, and the reward head inherits these biases \\textit{through the latent state} rather than learning them directly.  The reward head's large gradient contribution reflects sensitivity to $e_{\\Delta t}$'s parameters but not causal responsibility for the fidelity gap.\n\n\\subsubsection{Entropy Dynamics Across Configurations}\n\\label{sec:entropy_dynamics}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_entropy_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Actor entropy trajectories for three configurations. All start at ${\\sim}8$ nats and converge to similar values (${\\sim}{-}4.5$ to ${-}5$). The CIVO-triggering configuration (008d, red) follows a normal entropy reduction trajectory; the divergence from the control (008f) is subtle in entropy space, consistent with CIVO operating through the value channel rather than the entropy channel. 018v2 (Full-SGS, seed~1) shows slightly lower terminal entropy.}\n\\label{fig:entropy_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsubsection{Exp\\_016e Reinterpreted as Stochastic Variation}\n\\label{sec:016e_reinterp}\n\nThe gradient decomposition establishes that exp\\_016e targets the largest gradient pathway (reward: 38--49\\%) but not the informationally critical one (observation decoders: 16--22\\%).  The apparent timing differences between exp\\_016e and exp\\_008d (later collapse onset, 75K vs.\\ 25K, and higher peak evaluation, 326.7 vs.\\ 167.2) must be attributed to stochastic variation rather than SGS effects, since the critical observation decoder channel remains intact.  All three trajectories (exp\\_008d, exp\\_009b, exp\\_016e) share the invariant identified by the NVE diagnostic: each crosses the $1.5\\times$ pathological threshold at some point during training.\n\n\\subsection{Post-CIVO Outcomes: Why Seeds Diverge}\n\\label{sec:seed_divergence}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Post-CIVO outcomes for the 008d configuration. Peak/Final NVE measured in the 35K+ window.}\n\\label{tab:post_civo}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Peak} & \\textbf{Final} & \\textbf{Eval} & \\textbf{Outcome} \\\\\n\\midrule\ns1 (008d) & 4.12$\\times$ & 4.12$\\times$ & 82.8 & Full collapse \\\\\ns2 (009b) & 1.62$\\times$ & 0.38$\\times$ & 267.9 & Transient CIVO \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe two post-CIVO outcomes (Table~\\ref{tab:post_civo}), full collapse and transient CIVO with self-correction, both cross the $\\nve > 1.5\\times$ threshold, yet the downstream damage is stochastic.\\footnote{Exp\\_009e was excluded from multi-seed analyses due to a seed configuration error (default seed=0, identical to exp\\_008d).}  Bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10000$) over the $n{=}2$ effective seeds yields peak NVE mean $3.085$ (95\\% CI: $[2.05, 4.12]$), collapse rate $0.5$, and Cohen's $d = 2.60$ (mean difference $2.205$) relative to the no-embedding control, confirming a large effect despite the limited sample.  We hypothesize that the divergence relates to the critic's representational capacity at the time of CIVO onset.  \\citet{lyle2022capacity} demonstrate that deep RL networks suffer progressive capacity loss, in which early-learned features become increasingly difficult to overwrite.  If the critic's capacity is already partially consumed when CIVO injects overestimation, the network cannot recalibrate (seed~0); if sufficient capacity remains, the critic can absorb transient overestimation and recover (seed~2).\n\n\\subsection{Why MPC Is Immune}\n\\label{sec:mpc_immune}\n\n\\citet{nhu2025tawm} show that $\\dt$-conditioning benefits TD-MPC2, raising the question of why it fails in DreamerV3.  The critical difference is that MPC re-plans from real states at every step, so imagination errors do not accumulate into the training signal because there is no critic bootstrapping from imagined trajectories.  In DreamerV3, the actor-critic trains entirely within imagination, and any systematic reward-prediction bias at $\\dt > 1$ is amplified through the closed actor-critic loop.\n\n\\subsection{The \\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}-Embedding Dilemma}\n\\label{sec:embedding_dilemma}\n\nThe factorial results reveal a fundamental tension: removing the $\\dt$-embedding (008f) eliminates CIVO but at a $13\\times$ cost to reward prediction quality ($0.7469$ vs.\\ $0.0568$ MAE), as the world model cannot distinguish temporal scales.  Restricting imagination to $\\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding (008e\\_v3) avoids CIVO in 1 of 2 seeds but the other collapses via Pathway~B (\\S\\ref{sec:f6}).  Neither approach alone provides a complete solution.\n\n\\subsection{Multiple Vulnerability Pathways}\n\\label{sec:pathways}\n\nThe two collapse mechanisms identified in \\S\\ref{sec:f6}, CIVO (Pathway~A, value-driven, entropy decreasing) and non-CIVO instability (Pathway~B, eval-driven, entropy increasing), have distinct diagnostic signatures but a shared root cause: the trained $\\dt$-embedding's learned features affect both reward prediction across temporal scales (Pathway~A) and the representation space in which the actor operates (Pathway~B).  This explains why $\\nve$ is sensitive but not perfectly specific: Pathway~B's late evaluation decline inflates $\\nve$ through the same arithmetic as CIVO, yielding the sole false positive ($3.78\\times$ for 008e\\_v3 s2).  Distinguishing the two requires examining the temporal profile and entropy direction of collapse.\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Temporal Abstraction in World Models}\n\\label{sec:implications}\n\nCIVO identifies a structural incompatibility between flat temporal conditioning and imagination-based actor-critic training.  Hierarchical approaches (THICK \\citep{gumbsch2024thick}, Hieros \\citep{mattes2024hieros}, LS-Imagine \\citep{li2025lsimagine}) may avoid this by maintaining separate dynamics per temporal scale.  Whether CIVO transfers to transformer-based world models (DreamerV4; \\citealt{hafner2025dreamerv4}) depends on whether attention weights co-adapt with temporal embeddings analogously.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 358,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 358
    }
  }
}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01LzC1FV6mrtqZpmc1AJMpn6`</summary>

```text
1	Temporal abstraction via variable-rate imagination is a natural extension of world-model reinforcement learning, yet we find that every variable-rate configuration of DreamerV3 degrades performance, despite the success of identical $\Delta t$-conditioning in MPC-based methods. Through factorial diagnostics on two continuous-control tasks (Walker Walk, Cheetah Run), we identify the combination of trained temporal embedding and multi-rate data exposure as the causal factor and characterize the resulting failure mode: \emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO). The trained embedding enables the RSSM reward head to develop a $2.78\times$ prediction gap between $\Delta t{=}1$ and $\Delta t{=}2$, which the critic internalizes as optimistic values. This mechanism universally degrades performance to below 30\% of baseline across all three tested seeds ($n{=}3$), with two progressing toward full collapse and one self-correcting to a stable but degraded state. Gradient decomposition reveals that despite the reward head providing the largest gradient contribution (38--49\%), the observation decoder channel (16--22\%) carries the critical $\Delta t$-specific spatial information that drives the fidelity gap. Full-pathway gradient stopping partially prevents CIVO but succeeds in only 50\% of cases; this exposes additional mechanisms beyond gradient-mediated co-adaptation. A lightweight online detector that monitors value-to-evaluation ratio dynamics identifies CIVO onset with 100\% sensitivity (3/3 cases) and provides predictive warning before performance degradation in 2/3 cases, which supports early intervention without a reference baseline. The contrast with MPC-based methods identifies the feedback architecture (imagination-based bootstrapping versus re-planning) as the structural factor that transforms manageable model imprecision into pathological value dynamics.
2	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	TAWM \citep{nhu2025tawm} shows that conditioning a world model on step size $\dt$ yields consistent gains within TD-MPC2's MPC framework \citep{hansen2024tdmpc2}. Can the same $\dt$-conditioning bring temporal abstraction to imagination-based actor-critic methods like DreamerV3 \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, where policies are trained entirely in imagined rollouts?
5	
6	We find that it cannot. Every variable-rate configuration we test degrades performance catastrophically: fixed $\dt{=}2$ collapses to $14\%$ of baseline, fixed $\dt{=}4$ never exceeds $19\%$ of baseline, and a learned Gumbel-Softmax gate collapses to $11\%$. Through a factorial analysis on Walker Walk \citep{tassa2018dmcontrol}, we isolate the combination of a \emph{trained} $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as the causal factor within the factorial design tested. This trained-and-exposed embedding enables the RSSM reward head to develop a $2.78\times$ prediction gap between $\dt{=}1$ and $\dt{=}2$, which the critic internalizes as optimistic value estimates, a failure mode we term \emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO). A $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold (35K+ window) achieves 11/12 CIVO classification ($n{=}12$, single environment), with one non-CIVO false positive, a failure mode analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional} that arises from model imprecision amplified by learned temporal conditioning. Gradient decomposition reveals a non-obvious mechanism: the reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\%) but does not encode $\dt$-specific structural information, while the observation decoder channel (16--22\%) carries the critical spatial predictions that drive the RSSM fidelity gap. Reward-only gradient stopping fails (NVE $10.42\times$) despite targeting the largest pathway; full-pathway SGS that removes observation decoder gradients from the temporal embedding \sgsclaim{} (eval \sgseval{} vs.\ collapse at 40.8). This intervention is necessary but not sufficient, as the 50\% recovery rate exposes additional mechanisms beyond gradient-mediated co-adaptation.
7	
8	Four further findings complicate the picture: (1)~expanded seed analysis ($n{=}3$) confirms that the same CIVO trigger universally degrades performance to below 30\% of baseline, with 2/3 of seeds progressing toward full collapse at variable onset timing and 1/3 self-correcting to a stable but degraded state; (2)~restricting imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding does not prevent collapse, with one of two seeds failing via a qualitatively distinct non-CIVO pathway (evaluation-driven, increasing entropy); (3)~the contrast with TAWM identifies the feedback architecture (imagination-based bootstrapping versus MPC re-planning) as the structural factor that transforms manageable model imprecision into pathological value dynamics; and (4)~a lightweight four-condition rule-based monitor that tracks value-to-evaluation ratio dynamics achieves 100\% sensitivity for CIVO detection (3/3 cases) with predictive warning before performance degradation in 2/3 cases, which supports early intervention without a reference baseline run.
9	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around seven findings (F1--F7) that establish a causal chain from trained temporal conditioning to pathological value dynamics. All experiments use Walker Walk (DMC) with DreamerV3; treatments run to 105K steps and the baseline to 505K steps. We report evaluation returns (mean over 10 episodes) and the normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\nve$, \S\ref{sec:metrics}).

34	\end{tabular}
35	\vspace{1pt}
36	{\raggedright\footnotesize
37	trn = trained ($d{=}32$, subsample ON);
38	exp$^a$ = trained, subsample OFF;
39	frz$^b$ = frozen ($d{=}32$, \texttt{requires\_grad=False});
40	--- = removed ($d{=}0$).\\
41	$^c$ NVE normalized against exp\_019a\_v2 (vanilla DreamerV3 on PickCube-v1).\par}
42	\end{table*}
43	
44	%% ============================================================
45	%% F1: All dt>1 degrade
46	%% ============================================================
47	
48	\subsection{F1: All Variable-Rate Configurations Degrade}
49	\label{sec:f1}
50	
51	Every configuration that introduces $\dt > 1$ imagination degrades relative to the $\dt{=}1$ baseline (Figure~\ref{fig:learning_curves}). The baseline reaches 915.7 at 105K; fixed $\dt{=}2$ (006) peaks at 485.3 before collapsing to 132.9 ($14\%$ of baseline); fixed $\dt{=}4$ (007) never exceeds 176.9 ($19\%$); the learned gate (005) collapses to 98.8 ($11\%$).
52	
53	\begin{figure}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_learning_curves.pdf}
56	\caption{Learning curves. The baseline (black) reaches 915.7; all variable-rate configurations degrade.}
57	\label{fig:learning_curves}
58	\end{figure}
59	
60	%% ============================================================
61	%% F2: Trained dt_embedding → nve > 1.5x
62	%% ============================================================
63	
64	\subsection{F2: Trained Temporal Embedding Triggers Value Overestimation}
65	\label{sec:f2}
66	
67	The factorial design isolates trained $\dt$-embedding as the causal trigger. Comparing exp\_008d (trained, subsample ON) against exp\_008f (no embedding, subsample ON): 008d reaches $\nve = 4.12\times$ and collapses (eval 82.8), while 008f maintains $\nve \leq 0.97\times$ (eval 503.0). The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 11 of 12 experimental configurations (AUROC${=}0.926$; Figure~\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).\footnote{Excluding 3 baseline configurations, the 8-experiment subset yields accuracy$\,{=}\,$7/8 and AUROC$\,{=}\,$0.867.}
68	
69	\begin{figure}[t]
70	\centering
71	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}
72	\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\pm$ seed range). Trained $\dt$-embedding (red, $n{=}3$) exceeds the $1.5\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}
73	\label{fig:nve_trajectory}
74	\end{figure}
75	
76	The trained $\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in all three tested seeds (peak NVE: $4.12\times$, $1.62\times$, $1.53\times$; all $\geq 1.5\times$ threshold), universally degrading performance to below 30\% of baseline (mean eval 206.2, 22.5\% of baseline). Downstream trajectories diverge: seed~1 (008d) undergoes immediate full collapse (eval 82.8, 9.0\% of baseline), seed~3 (008d\_s3) exhibits late-onset collapse still in progress at 105K (eval 268, train returns declining 70\% over final 8K steps), while seed~2 (009b) self-corrects after transient overestimation (eval 267.9, 29.3\% of baseline). This pattern establishes universal CIVO onset with stochastic downstream resolution: collapse is the majority outcome (2/3 seeds) but exhibits variable onset timing (35K vs 70K steps). The 1/3 self-correction case remains severely degraded. This confirms that CIVO universally damages performance regardless of whether full collapse follows (Appendix~\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).
77	
78	%% ============================================================
79	%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)
80	%% ============================================================
81	
82	\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}
83	\label{sec:f3f5_summary}
84	
85	Trained $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure are \emph{jointly necessary} for CIVO. Neither factor alone triggers pathological overestimation: trained embedding without multi-rate exposure (008a, NVE $= 0.93\times$) and multi-rate exposure without embedding (008f, NVE $= 0.97\times$) both remain well below the $1.5\times$ diagnostic threshold, while frozen embedding under full exposure (015b) peaks near the diagnostic threshold ($1.45\times$ at 55K) but retreats to $0.55\times$ by 105K; frozen embedding thus prevents the sustained escalation characteristic of CIVO (Figure~\ref{fig:factorial_grid}). Degradation scales monotonically with $\dt$ ($-47\%$ at $\dt{=}2$, $-81\%$ at $\dt{=}4$; Figure~\ref{fig:dt_amplification}).
86	
87	%% ============================================================
88	%% F6--F7: Interventions and cross-environment
89	%% ============================================================
90	
91	\subsection{F6--F7: Interventions and Multiple Pathways}
92	\label{sec:f6}
93	
94	\paragraph{F6: $\dt{=}1$ imagination does not prevent all collapse.}
95	Exp\_008e\_v3 restricts imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding. Seed~1 is healthy (eval 869.9, $\nve = 0.94\times$); seed~2 collapses (eval $715 \rightarrow 137$, $\nve = 3.78\times$), but via a qualitatively distinct mechanism (Figure~\ref{fig:multiple_pathways}). Classic CIVO (008d, Pathway~A) shows value explosion followed by evaluation decline with decreasing entropy; 008e\_v3\_s2 (Pathway~B) shows evaluation crash with \emph{increasing} entropy ($-5.21 \rightarrow -3.83$) while values remain stable. This produces the sole false positive in the NVE classification.
96	
97	\begin{figure}[t]
98	\centering
99	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig6_multiple_pathways.pdf}
100	\caption{Contrasting collapse dynamics. \textbf{Left:} 008d (CIVO), value explosion with entropy decrease. \textbf{Right:} 008e\_v3 s2 (non-CIVO), evaluation crash with entropy increase.}
101	\label{fig:multiple_pathways}
102	\end{figure}
103	
104	\paragraph{F7: Full-pathway gradient stopping shows mixed results.}
105	Exp\_018 applies Selective Gradient Stopping (SGS) by detaching all non-KL gradients from $e_{\dt}$; this blocks the observation decoder pathway identified in F2 as the primary CIVO conduit. Figure~\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions. Trained $\dt$-embedding alone produces $-73\%$ peak and $-86\%$ final eval degradation relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 avoids collapse (eval \sgseval{}, $-51\%$ final relative to baseline) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8, $-96\%$); the overall recovery rate is 50\%. SGS reduces but does not eliminate CIVO risk: the gradient pathway it blocks is necessary but not sufficient for reliable elimination. Reward-only SGS (016e) fails entirely (NVE $10.42\times$). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (010b) confirms CIVO generalization: evaluation peaks at 321.9 before declining to 51.7 at 105K ($-84\%$, minimum 22.7 at 65K); peak NVE $8.27\times$. Full-SGS on Cheetah Run enables recovery from transient CIVO onset (NVE $9.94\times$ at 45K, eval 4.6) to functional performance (eval 221.5 at 105K), in contrast to permanent collapse without SGS (010b: eval 51.7). Combined with Walker results (50\% recovery rate), SGS is necessary but not sufficient for CIVO elimination. Removing the embedding on Cheetah eliminates CIVO entirely (024b, 2 seeds, NVE $< 0.2\times$, eval 96.5/77.3), replicating the Walker causal control (008f) in a second environment.
106	
107	\begin{figure}[t]
108	\centering
109	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}
110	\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk (DMC) across four conditions. \textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3, no temporal embedding. \textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\footnotemark: DreamerV3 with a learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding exposed to multi-rate data. \textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with Selective Gradient Stopping that detaches all non-KL gradients from $e_{\dt}$, two seeds. Trained $\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\%$ and final eval by $86\%$ relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 partially recovers ($-51\%$ final, no collapse) but seed~2 collapses ($-96\%$ final, marked ``collapsed''), indicating that SGS enables recovery from CIVO in some seeds but is not sufficient for reliable prevention (recovery rate $= 50\%$). All percentages relative to Baseline 001.}
111	\label{fig:civo_decomposition}
112	\end{figure}
113	\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}
114

116	%% TABLE 2: RSSM Fidelity
117	%% ============================================================
118	
119	\begin{table}[t]
120	\centering
121	\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\dt{=}1$ vs.\ $\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}
122	\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}
123	\footnotesize
124	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
125	\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}

127	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{obs\_MSE}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{reward\_MAE}} \\
```
