```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	TAWM \citep{nhu2025tawm} shows that conditioning a world model on step size $\dt$ yields consistent gains within TD-MPC2's MPC framework \citep{hansen2024tdmpc2}. Can the same $\dt$-conditioning bring temporal abstraction to imagination-based actor-critic methods like DreamerV3 \citep{hafner2023dreamerv3}, where policies are trained entirely in imagined rollouts?
5	
6	We find that it cannot. Every variable-rate configuration we test degrades performance catastrophically: fixed $\dt{=}2$ collapses to $14\%$ of baseline, fixed $\dt{=}4$ never exceeds $19\%$ of baseline, and a learned Gumbel-Softmax gate collapses to $11\%$. Through a factorial analysis on Walker Walk \citep{tassa2018dmcontrol}, we isolate the combination of a \emph{trained} $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure as the causal factor within the factorial design tested. This trained-and-exposed embedding enables the RSSM reward head to develop a $2.78\times$ prediction gap between $\dt{=}1$ and $\dt{=}2$, which the critic internalizes as optimistic value estimates, a failure mode we term \emph{conditioning-induced value overestimation} (CIVO). A $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold (35K+ window) achieves 11/12 CIVO classification ($n{=}12$, single environment), with one non-CIVO false positive, a failure mode analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional} that arises from model imprecision amplified by learned temporal conditioning. Gradient decomposition reveals a non-obvious mechanism: the reward head contributes the largest gradient share (38--49\%) but does not encode $\dt$-specific structural information, while the observation decoder channel (16--22\%) carries the critical spatial predictions that drive the RSSM fidelity gap. Reward-only gradient stopping fails (NVE $10.42\times$) despite targeting the largest pathway; full-pathway SGS that removes observation decoder gradients from the temporal embedding \sgsclaim{} (eval \sgseval{} vs.\ collapse at 40.8). This intervention is necessary but not sufficient, as the 50\% recovery rate exposes additional mechanisms beyond gradient-mediated co-adaptation.
7	
8	Four further findings complicate the picture: (1)~expanded seed analysis ($n{=}3$) confirms that the same CIVO trigger universally degrades performance to below 30\% of baseline, with 2/3 of seeds progressing toward full collapse at variable onset timing and 1/3 self-correcting to a stable but degraded state; (2)~restricting imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding does not prevent collapse, with one of two seeds failing via a qualitatively distinct non-CIVO pathway (evaluation-driven, increasing entropy); (3)~the contrast with TAWM identifies the feedback architecture (imagination-based bootstrapping versus MPC re-planning) as the structural factor that transforms manageable model imprecision into pathological value dynamics; and (4)~a lightweight four-condition rule-based monitor that tracks value-to-evaluation ratio dynamics achieves 100\% sensitivity for CIVO detection (3/3 cases) with predictive warning before performance degradation in 2/3 cases, which supports early intervention without a reference baseline run.
9	
10	\paragraph{Contributions.}
11	\begin{itemize}
12	    \item We identify the feedback architecture (imagination-based bootstrapping versus MPC re-planning) as the structural factor that determines whether $\dt$-conditioning is beneficial or pathological, explaining why identical conditioning succeeds in TD-MPC2 but fails in DreamerV3.
13	\end{itemize}
14	
```

```text
64	\subsection{F2: Trained Temporal Embedding Triggers Value Overestimation}
65	\label{sec:f2}
66	
67	The factorial design isolates trained $\dt$-embedding as the causal trigger. Comparing exp\_008d (trained, subsample ON) against exp\_008f (no embedding, subsample ON): 008d reaches $\nve = 4.12\times$ and collapses (eval 82.8), while 008f maintains $\nve \leq 0.97\times$ (eval 503.0). The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 11 of 12 experimental configurations (AUROC${=}0.926$; Figure~\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).\footnote{Excluding 3 baseline configurations, the 8-experiment subset yields accuracy$\,{=}\,$7/8 and AUROC$\,{=}\,$0.867.}
68	
69	\begin{figure}[t]
70	\centering
71	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}
72	\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\pm$ seed range). Trained $\dt$-embedding (red, $n{=}3$) exceeds the $1.5\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}
73	\label{fig:nve_trajectory}
74	\end{figure}
75	
76	The trained $\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in all three tested seeds (peak NVE: $4.12\times$, $1.62\times$, $1.53\times$; all $\geq 1.5\times$ threshold), universally degrading performance to below 30\% of baseline (mean eval 206.2, 22.5\% of baseline). Downstream trajectories diverge: seed~1 (008d) undergoes immediate full collapse (eval 82.8, 9.0\% of baseline), seed~3 (008d\_s3) exhibits late-onset collapse still in progress at 105K (eval 268, train returns declining 70\% over final 8K steps), while seed~2 (009b) self-corrects after transient overestimation (eval 267.9, 29.3\% of baseline). This pattern establishes universal CIVO onset with stochastic downstream resolution: collapse is the majority outcome (2/3 seeds) but exhibits variable onset timing (35K vs 70K steps). The 1/3 self-correction case remains severely degraded. This confirms that CIVO universally damages performance regardless of whether full collapse follows (Appendix~\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).
77	
78	%% ============================================================
79	%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)
80	%% ============================================================
81	
82	\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}
83	\label{sec:f3f5_summary}

The $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 11 of 12 experimental configurations (AUROC${=}0.926$; Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).\\footnote{Excluding 3 baseline configurations, the 8-experiment subset yields accuracy$\\,{=}\\,$7/8 and AUROC$\\,{=}\\,$0.867.}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\\pm$ seed range). Trained $\\dt$-embedding (red, $n{=}3$) exceeds the $1.5\\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}\n\\label{fig:nve_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe trained $\\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in all three tested seeds (peak NVE: $4.12\\times$, $1.62\\times$, $1.53\\times$; all $\\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold), universally degrading performance to below 30\\% of baseline (mean eval 206.2, 22.5\\% of baseline). Downstream trajectories diverge: seed~1 (008d) undergoes immediate full collapse (eval 82.8, 9.0\\% of baseline), seed~3 (008d\\_s3) exhibits late-onset collapse still in progress at 105K (eval 268, train returns declining 70\\% over final 8K steps), while seed~2 (009b) self-corrects after transient overestimation (eval 267.9, 29.3\\% of baseline). This pattern establishes universal CIVO onset with stochastic downstream resolution: collapse is the majority outcome (2/3 seeds) but exhibits variable onset timing (35K vs 70K steps). The 1/3 self-correction case remains severely degraded. This confirms that CIVO universally damages performance regardless of whether full collapse follows (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}\n\\label{sec:f3f5_summary}",

Comparing exp\\_008d (trained, subsample ON) against exp\\_008f (no embedding, subsample ON): 008d reaches $\\nve = 4.12\\times$ and collapses (eval 82.8), while 008f maintains $\\nve \\leq 0.97\\times$ (eval 503.0). The $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 11 of 12 experimental configurations (AUROC${=}0.926$; Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).\\footnote{Excluding 3 baseline configurations, the 8-experiment subset yields accuracy$\\,{=}\\,$7/8 and AUROC$\\,{=}\\,$0.867.}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\\pm$ seed range). Trained $\\dt$-embedding (red, $n{=}3$) exceeds the $1.5\\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}\n\\label{fig:nve_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe trained $\\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in all three tested seeds (peak NVE: $4.12\\times$, $1.62\\times$, $1.53\\times$; all $\\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold), universally degrading performance to below 30\\% of baseline (mean eval 206.2, 22.5\\% of baseline). Downstream trajectories diverge: seed~1 (008d) undergoes immediate full collapse (eval 82.8, 9.0\\% of baseline), seed~3 (008d\\_s3) exhibits late-onset collapse still in progress at 105K (eval 268, train returns declining 70\\% over final 8K steps), while seed~2 (009b) self-corrects after transient overestimation (eval 267.9, 29.3\\% of baseline). This pattern establishes universal CIVO onset with stochastic downstream resolution: collapse is the majority outcome (2/3 seeds) but exhibits variable onset timing (35K vs 70K steps). The 1/3 self-correction case remains severely degraded. This confirms that CIVO universally damages performance regardless of whether full collapse follows (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}\n\\label{sec:f3f5_summary}\n\nTrained $\\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure are \\emph{jointly necessary} for CIVO. Neither factor alone triggers pathological overestimation: trained embedding without multi-rate exposure (008a, NVE $= 0.93\\times$) and multi-rate exposure without embedding (008f, NVE $= 0.97\\times$) both remain well below the $1.5\\times$ diagnostic threshold, while frozen embedding under full exposure (015b) peaks near the diagnostic threshold ($1.45\\times$ at 55K) but retreats to $0.55\\times$ by 105K; frozen embedding thus prevents the sustained escalation characteristic of CIVO (Figure~\\ref{fig:factorial_grid}). Degradation scales monotonically with $\\dt$ ($-47\\%$ at $\\dt{=}2$, $-81\\%$ at $\\dt{=}4$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dt_amplification}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F6--F7: Interventions and cross-environment\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F6--F7: Interventions and Multiple Pathways}\n\\label{sec:f6}\n\n\\paragraph{F6: $\\dt{=}1$ imagination does not prevent all collapse.}\nExp\\_008e\\_v3 restricts imagination to $\\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding. Seed~1 is healthy (eval 869.9, $\\nve = 0.94\\times$); seed~2 collapses (eval $715 \\rightarrow 137$, $\\nve = 3.78\\times$), but via a qualitatively distinct mechanism (Figure~\\ref{fig:multiple_pathways}). Classic CIVO (008d, Pathway~A) shows value explosion followed by evaluation decline with decreasing entropy; 008e\\_v3\\_s2 (Pathway~B) shows evaluation crash with \\emph{increasing} entropy ($-5.21 \\rightarrow -3.83$) while values remain stable. This produces the sole false positive in the NVE classification.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig6_multiple_pathways.pdf}\n\\caption{Contrasting collapse dynamics. \\textbf{Left:} 008d (CIVO), value explosion with entropy decrease. \\textbf{Right:} 008e\\_v3 s2 (non-CIVO), evaluation crash with entropy increase.}\n\\label{fig:multiple_pathways}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{F7: Full-pathway gradient stopping shows mixed results.}\nExp\\_018 applies Selective Gradient Stopping (SGS) by detaching all non-KL gradients from $e_{\\dt}$; this blocks the observation decoder pathway identified in F2 as the primary CIVO conduit. Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions. Trained $\\dt$-embedding alone produces $-73\\%$ peak and $-86\\%$ final eval degradation relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 avoids collapse (eval \\sgseval{}, $-51\\%$ final relative to baseline) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8, $-96\\%$); the overall recovery rate is 50\\%. SGS reduces but does not eliminate CIVO risk: the gradient pathway it blocks is necessary but not sufficient for reliable elimination. Reward-only SGS (016e) fails entirely (NVE $10.42\\times$). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (010b) confirms CIVO generalization: evaluation peaks at 321.9 before declining to 51.7 at 105K ($-84\\%$, minimum 22.7 at 65K); peak NVE $8.27\\times$. Full-SGS on Cheetah Run enables recovery from transient CIVO onset (NVE $9.94\\times$ at 45K, eval 4.6) to functional performance (eval 221.5 at 105K), in contrast to permanent collapse without SGS (010b: eval 51.7). Combined with Walker results (50\\% recovery rate), SGS is necessary but not sufficient for CIVO elimination. Removing the embedding on Cheetah eliminates CIVO entirely (024b, 2 seeds, NVE $< 0.2\\times$, eval 96.5/77.3), replicating the Walker causal control (008f) in a second environment.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk (DMC) across four conditions. \\textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3, no temporal embedding. \\textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\\footnotemark: DreamerV3 with a learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding exposed to multi-rate data. \\textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with Selective Gradient Stopping that detaches all non-KL gradients from $e_{\\dt}$, two seeds. Trained $\\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\\%$ and final eval by $86\\%$ relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 partially recovers ($-51\\%$ final, no collapse) but seed~2 collapses ($-96\\%$ final, marked ``collapsed''), indicating that SGS enables recovery from CIVO in some seeds but is not sufficient for reliable prevention (recovery rate $= 50\\%$). All percentages relative to Baseline 001.}\n\\label{fig:civo_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% TABLE 2: RSSM Fidelity\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\\dt{=}1$ vs.\\ $\\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}\n\\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{obs\\_MSE}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{reward\\_MAE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\\times$ \\\\\n008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\\times$ \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe trained embedding in 008d produces a $2.78\\times$ reward prediction gap between $\\dt{=}1$ and $\\dt{=}2$ (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}). This systematic bias is internalized by the critic as optimistic value estimates. Having established the mechanistic basis of CIVO, we now ask whether it can be detected during training without access to a reference baseline.\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F8: Online CIVO Detection\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{Online CIVO Detection}\n\\label{sec:detector}\n\n\\paragraph{Motivation.}\nThe Normalized Value Excess ($\\nve$) quantifies CIVO severity by comparing the value-to-evaluation ratio against a reference baseline that does not employ temporal abstraction. This comparison is informative for post-hoc diagnosis but unusable during deployment: no practitioner has access to a converged baseline run while training a new agent. A metric that requires a completed control experiment cannot serve as an early warning.\n\nWe therefore ask whether the \\emph{dynamics} of value overestimation, rather than its absolute magnitude relative to an external reference, contain sufficient signal to detect CIVO onset in real time. By tracking the trajectory of the value-to-evaluation ratio $r_t = \\bar{V}_t / \\max(\\bar{E}_t, \\varepsilon)$ (with $\\varepsilon = 10^{-6}$ for numerical stability) across checkpoints, a lightweight rule-based detector can flag dangerous overestimation using only quantities available within a single run.\n\nThe causal structure of CIVO motivates acceleration as the key detection signal. CIVO operates as a positive feedback loop: the trained embedding biases reward predictions, which inflate value estimates, which in turn distort actor gradients toward trajectories that reinforce the bias (\\S\\ref{sec:civo_mechanism}). This self-amplifying cycle produces superlinear growth in $r_t$. In healthy training, a natural negative feedback counteracts value growth: improved value estimates yield a better actor, which raises evaluation returns and stabilizes the ratio. CIVO breaks this negative feedback, so sustained positive acceleration in $r_t$ serves as a causal signature that distinguishes pathological divergence from normal value learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Algorithm.}\nThe detector monitors $r_t$ and fires an alert only when four conditions hold simultaneously at checkpoint $t$ (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:civo_detector}). The conjunction makes the detector conservative: any single noisy checkpoint is unlikely to satisfy magnitude, velocity, acceleration, and decline together. The computational cost is negligible (four scalar comparisons per checkpoint, no additional model inference).\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Online CIVO Detector}\n\\label{alg:civo_detector}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Stream of $(\\bar{V}_t, \\bar{E}_t)$ at each checkpoint $t$\n\\STATE $\\tau_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow 0.49$, \\quad $\\tau_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow 0.1$, \\quad $\\varepsilon \\leftarrow 10^{-6}$\n\\STATE Initialize $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\mathcal{H} \\leftarrow []$\n\\FOR{each checkpoint $t$}\n  \\STATE $r_t \\leftarrow \\bar{V}_t \\,/\\, \\max(\\bar{E}_t,\\, \\varepsilon)$\n  \\STATE $\\Delta r_t \\leftarrow r_t - r_\\text{prev}$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow (r_t \\geq \\tau_\\text{mag})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_t > \\tau_\\text{vel})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{acc} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_\\text{prev} > \\tau_\\text{vel}) \\wedge (\\Delta r_t > \\Delta r_\\text{prev})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{dec} \\leftarrow \\bigl|\\{i : \\mathcal{H}[i] < \\mathcal{H}[i{-}1],\\; i \\in \\text{last 3}\\}\\bigr| \\geq 2$\n  \\IF{$c_\\text{mag} \\wedge c_\\text{vel} \\wedge c_\\text{acc} \\wedge c_\\text{dec}$}\n    \\STATE \\textbf{Alert:} CIVO onset at step $t$\n  \\ENDIF\n  \\STATE $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow r_t$, \\quad $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow \\Delta r_t$\n  \\STATE Append $\\bar{E}_t$ to $\\mathcal{H}$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe four conditions capture complementary signals: (1)~\\emph{Magnitude} ($r_t \\geq 0.49$) confirms that the value-to-evaluation ratio has reached a level indicative of overestimation; (2)~\\emph{Velocity} ($\\Delta r_t > 0.1$) rules out slow drift or plateau behavior; (3)~\\emph{Acceleration} ($\\Delta r_t > \\Delta r_{t-1}$, both $> 0.1$) filters oscillatory paths where the ratio rises and falls without sustained divergence; (4)~\\emph{Evaluation decline} ($\\geq 2$ of 3 recent checkpoints decline) confirms that overestimation coincides with actual performance degradation. A 2-of-3 majority threshold balances noise robustness against single-checkpoint evaluation variance with sensitivity to sustained decline.\\footnote{A full comparison of window sizes (3/3, 2/3, 2/5) and their trade-offs appears in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sensitivity}.}\n\nSensitivity analysis over $\\tau_\\text{mag} \\in [0.30, 0.70]$ confirms that $\\tau_\\text{mag} = 0.49$ maximizes Youden's $J = 0.714$; raising the threshold to $0.50$ loses one true positive (seed~3 triggers at $r_t = 0.497$) with no reduction in false positives, since both false alarms have $r_t > 0.70$ at trigger time. The velocity threshold $\\tau_\\text{vel} = 0.1$ is stable across $[0.05, 0.18]$ (TPR remains $3/3$); we retain $0.1$ to avoid overfitting to $n{=}11$ runs.\n\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\nWe apply the detector retrospectively to eleven runs: trained duration embedding with sustained overestimation (CIVO-positive, $n{=}3$), untrained or absent embedding (CIVO-negative, $n{=}3$), vanilla baselines ($n{=}4$), and one cross-simulator case (PickCube-v1 in ManiSkill3, trained embedding). Ground truth labels derive from manual inspection: a run is CIVO-positive if it exhibits sustained $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ with subsequent evaluation collapse. Table~\\ref{tab:detector-validation} reports all results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Retrospective CIVO detector validation ($n{=}11$). Detection type: ``predictive'' = alert precedes 20\\% eval decline; ``reactive'' = alert follows decline onset.}\n\\label{tab:detector-validation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Exp} & \\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Verdict} & \\textbf{Trigger} & \\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Peak Eval} & $r_\\text{ve}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d s0 & trained $\\dt$-emb & TP & 45K & predict. & 167 & 0.69 \\\\\n010b & trained $\\dt$-emb (Cheetah) & TP & 65K & reactive & 322 & 3.18 \\\\\n008d s3 & trained $\\dt$-emb & TP & 45K & predict. & 452 & 0.50 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008f s3 & no $\\dt$-emb & TN & -- & -- & 594 & -- \\\\\n009b s2 & trained $\\dt$-emb (self-corr.) & TN & -- & -- & 279 & -- \\\\\nbaseline s1 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 970 & -- \\\\\n009d s0 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 951 & -- \\\\\n009d s1 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 920 & -- \\\\\n019b & trained $\\dt$-emb (PickCube) & TN & -- & -- & 26.6 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n009c s2 & no $\\dt$-emb & FP & 105K & reactive & 359 & 0.75 \\\\\n009a s2 & vanilla & FP & 85K & predict. & 967 & 0.73 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{TPR = 3/3 (1.00), \\quad FPR = 2/8 (0.25)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nThe detector identifies all three CIVO-positive runs (TPR = 3/3). Two of three are detected \\emph{predictively}: 008d seed~0 triggers at 45K, one checkpoint before evaluation crosses the 20\\% decline threshold; seed~3 triggers at 45K, a full 50K steps before decline begins. The third case (010b, Cheetah Run) involves single-step collapse (evaluation drops 80\\% in one interval), and the detector fires reactively at the next available checkpoint. No checkpoint-based monitor can provide advance warning for instantaneous collapse.\n\nTwo false positives occur among eight negative cases (FPR = 2/8, precision = 3/5 = 0.60, F$_1$ = 0.75). Run 009c (no duration embedding) develops genuine late-stage value overestimation unrelated to CIVO; the detector correctly identifies value-performance dissociation, and the alert is arguably useful despite the label mismatch. Run 009a (vanilla baseline) triggers during a transient three-checkpoint eval dip at 85K; the model subsequently recovers to 966, constituting a pure false alarm. In practice, the cost of a false alert (manual checkpoint inspection, minutes) is orders of magnitude lower than the cost of undetected collapse (wasted GPU-hours, corrupted model); moderate FPR is therefore acceptable in this application.\n\nAll three true positives triggered before 65K steps (range 45--65K) while both false positives occurred after 85K (range 85--105K), suggesting that early-training alerts carry higher diagnostic confidence. In practice, alerts before 70K steps can be treated as high-confidence CIVO indicators; later alerts warrant manual inspection.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-simulator specificity (PickCube).}\nExp\\_019b applies the CIVO-trigger configuration (trained $\\dt$-embedding, subsample ON, $H{=}8$) to PickCube-v1 in ManiSkill3, a manipulation task with sparse rewards and qualitatively different dynamics from DMC locomotion. The V/E ratio exhibits an oscillatory pattern rather than monotonic divergence: it spikes to $r_t = 5.66$ at 52.5K (eval crashes from 24.9 to 3.7), then deflates to 1.38 by 72.5K as value estimates self-correct, eventually reaching 0.065$\\times$ NVE at 202.5K. This spike-then-deflation trajectory prevents the acceleration condition from co-occurring with evaluation decline at any single checkpoint. When V/E accelerates during early training (22.5--32.5K, consecutive velocities $+0.50$, $+1.85$), evaluation has not yet declined (C4 fails). When evaluation does decline (42.5--52.5K), the preceding V/E velocity is negative ($-0.81$), so the acceleration condition (C3) fails. Across all 14 checkpoints evaluated through 132.5K, no checkpoint satisfies all four conditions simultaneously, and the detector correctly remains silent (TN). This confirms that the acceleration filter distinguishes transient overestimation spikes from the sustained monotonic divergence characteristic of CIVO. The result extends the detector's specificity validation to a second simulator and a sparse-reward manipulation domain.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nFour constraints bound the current detector. First, the three-checkpoint window cannot distinguish transient dips from sustained collapse (the source of the 009a false alarm). Second, the acceleration condition assumes monotonically increasing overestimation during onset; oscillatory trajectories that eventually collapse could evade detection until acceleration stabilizes. Third, the detector identifies a \\emph{pattern} (rising value-evaluation ratio with declining performance) rather than a \\emph{mechanism}: run 009c shows that non-CIVO pathologies produce the same signature. Fourth, threshold values ($\\tau_\\text{mag} = 0.49$, $\\tau_\\text{vel} = 0.1$) were calibrated on eleven runs across three environments; generalization to different reward scales requires further validation. The detector targets sustained CIVO patterns; transient single-checkpoint spikes (as observed in SGS-protected runs) are by design not flagged, as they do not indicate impending permanent collapse.\n",

          " \\end{figure}",
          " ",
          "-The trained $\\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in all three tested seeds (peak NVE: $4.12\\times$, $1.62\\times$, $1.53\\times$; all $\\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold), universally degrading performance to below 30\\% of baseline (mean eval 206.2, 22.5\\% of baseline). Downstream trajectories diverge: seed~1 (008d) undergoes immediate full collapse (eval 82.8, 9.0\\% of baseline), seed~3 (008d\\_s3) exhibits late-onset collapse still in progress at 105K (eval 268, train returns declining 70\\% over final 8K steps), while seed~2 (009b) self-corrects after transient overestimation (eval 267.9, 29.3\\% of baseline). This pattern establishes universal CIVO onset with stochastic downstream resolution: collapse is the majority outcome (2/3 seeds) but exhibits variable onset timing (35K vs 70K steps). The 1/3 self-correction case remains severely degraded. This confirms that CIVO universally damages performance regardless of whether full collapse follows (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).",
          "+The trained $\\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in all four tested seeds (peak NVE: $4.12\\times$, $3.03\\times$, $1.62\\times$, $1.53\\times$; all $\\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold), universally degrading performance to below 30\\% of baseline (mean eval 170.6, 18.6\\% of baseline). Downstream trajectories diverge: seeds~1 and~4 (008d, 008d\\_s4) undergo full collapse (eval 82.8 and 63.6), seed~3 (008d\\_s3) exhibits late-onset collapse still in progress at 105K (eval 268, train returns declining 70\\% over final 8K steps), while seed~2 (009b) self-corrects after transient overestimation (eval 267.9, 29.3\\% of baseline). This pattern establishes universal CIVO onset with stochastic downstream resolution: collapse is the majority outcome (3/4 seeds) with variable onset timing (35K to 85K steps). The 1/4 self-correction case remains severely degraded. This confirms that CIVO universally damages performance regardless of whether full collapse follows (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).",

100	\caption{Contrasting collapse dynamics. \textbf{Left:} 008d (CIVO), value explosion with entropy decrease. \textbf{Right:} 008e\_v3 s2 (non-CIVO), evaluation crash with entropy increase.}
101	\label{fig:multiple_pathways}
102	\end{figure}
103	
104	\paragraph{F7: Full-pathway gradient stopping shows mixed results.}
105	Exp\_018 applies Selective Gradient Stopping (SGS) by detaching all non-KL gradients from $e_{\dt}$; this blocks the observation decoder pathway identified in F2 as the primary CIVO conduit. Figure~\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions. Trained $\dt$-embedding alone produces $-73\%$ peak and $-86\%$ final eval degradation relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 avoids collapse (eval \sgseval{}, $-51\%$ final relative to baseline) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8, $-96\%$); the overall recovery rate is 50\%. SGS reduces but does not eliminate CIVO risk: the gradient pathway it blocks is necessary but not sufficient for reliable elimination. Reward-only SGS (016e) fails entirely (NVE $10.42\times$). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (010b) confirms CIVO generalization: evaluation peaks at 321.9 before declining to 51.7 at 105K ($-84\%$, minimum 22.7 at 65K); peak NVE $8.27\times$. Full-SGS on Cheetah Run enables recovery from transient CIVO onset (NVE $9.94\times$ at 45K, eval 4.6) to functional performance (eval 221.5 at 105K), in contrast to permanent collapse without SGS (010b: eval 51.7). Combined with Walker results (50\% recovery rate), SGS is necessary but not sufficient for CIVO elimination. Removing the embedding on Cheetah eliminates CIVO entirely (024b, 2 seeds, NVE $< 0.2\times$, eval 96.5/77.3), replicating the Walker causal control (008f) in a second environment.
106	
107	\begin{figure}[t]
108	\centering
109	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}
110	\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk (DMC) across four conditions. \textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3, no temporal embedding. \textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\footnotemark: DreamerV3 with a learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding exposed to multi-rate data. \textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with Selective Gradient Stopping that detaches all non-KL gradients from $e_{\dt}$, two seeds. Trained $\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\%$ and final eval by $86\%$ relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 partially recovers ($-51\%$ final, no collapse) but seed~2 collapses ($-96\%$ final, marked ``collapsed''), indicating that SGS enables recovery from CIVO in some seeds but is not sufficient for reliable prevention (recovery rate $= 50\%$). All percentages relative to Baseline 001.}
111	\label{fig:civo_decomposition}
112	\end{figure}
113	\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}
114

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around seven findings (F1--F7) that establish a causal chain from trained temporal conditioning to pathological value dynamics. We report evaluation returns (mean over 10 episodes) and the normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\\nve$, \\S\\ref{sec:metrics}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% TABLE 1: Main results summary\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Key configurations and outcomes. The baseline reaches 915.7 at 105K; fixed $\\dt{=}2$ (006) peaks at 485.3 before collapsing to 132.9 ($14\\%$ of baseline); fixed $\\dt{=}4$ (007) never exceeds 176.9 ($19\\%$); the learned gate (005) collapses to 98.8 ($11\\%$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_learning_curves.pdf}\n\\caption{Learning curves. The baseline (black) reaches 915.7; all variable-rate configurations degrade.}\n\\label{fig:learning_curves}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F2: Trained dt_embedding → nve > 1.5x\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F2: Trained Temporal Embedding Triggers Value Overestimation}\n\\label{sec:f2}\n\nThe factorial design isolates trained $\\dt$-embedding as the causal trigger. Comparing exp\\_008d (trained, subsample ON) against exp\\_008f (no embedding, subsample ON): 008d reaches $\\nve = 4.12\\times$ and collapses (eval 82.8), while 008f maintains $\\nve \\leq 0.97\\times$ (eval 503.0). The $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 11 of 12 experimental configurations (AUROC${=}0.926$; Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).\\footnote{Excluding 3 baseline configurations, the 8-experiment subset yields accuracy$\\,{=}\\,$7/8 and AUROC$\\,{=}\\,$0.867.}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\\pm$ seed range). Trained $\\dt$-embedding (red, $n{=}3$) exceeds the $1.5\\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}\n\\label{fig:nve_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe trained $\\dt$-embedding triggers CIVO in all four tested seeds (peak NVE: $4.12\\times$, $3.03\\times$, $1.62\\times$, $1.53\\times$; all $\\geq 1.5\\times$ threshold), universally degrading performance to below 30\\% of baseline (mean eval 170.6, 18.6\\% of baseline). Downstream trajectories diverge: seeds~1 and~4 (008d, 008d\\_s4) undergo full collapse (eval 82.8 and 63.6), seed~3 (008d\\_s3) exhibits late-onset collapse still in progress at 105K (eval 268, train returns declining 70\\% over final 8K steps), while seed~2 (009b) self-corrects after transient overestimation (eval 267.9, 29.3\\% of baseline). This pattern establishes universal CIVO onset with stochastic downstream resolution: collapse is the majority outcome (3/4 seeds) with variable onset timing (35K to 85K steps). The 1/4 self-correction case remains severely degraded. This confirms that CIVO universally damages performance regardless of whether full collapse follows (Appendix~\\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}\n\\label{sec:f3f5_summary}\n\nTrained $\\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure are \\emph{jointly necessary} for CIVO. Neither factor alone triggers pathological overestimation: trained embedding without multi-rate exposure (008a, NVE $= 0.93\\times$) and multi-rate exposure without embedding (008f, NVE $= 0.97\\times$) both remain well below the $1.5\\times$ diagnostic threshold, while frozen embedding under full exposure (015b) peaks near the diagnostic threshold ($1.45\\times$ at 55K) but retreats to $0.55\\times$ by 105K; frozen embedding thus prevents the sustained escalation characteristic of CIVO (Figure~\\ref{fig:factorial_grid}). Degradation scales monotonically with $\\dt$ ($-47\\%$ at $\\dt{=}2$, $-81\\%$ at $\\dt{=}4$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dt_amplification}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F6--F7: Interventions and cross-environment\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{F6--F7: Interventions and Multiple Pathways}\n\\label{sec:f6}\n\n\\paragraph{F6: $\\dt{=}1$ imagination does not prevent all collapse.}\nExp\\_008e\\_v3 restricts imagination to $\\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding. Seed~1 is healthy (eval 869.9, $\\nve = 0.94\\times$); seed~2 collapses (eval $715 \\rightarrow 137$, $\\nve = 3.78\\times$), but via a qualitatively distinct mechanism (Figure~\\ref{fig:multiple_pathways}). Classic CIVO (008d, Pathway~A) shows value explosion followed by evaluation decline with decreasing entropy; 008e\\_v3\\_s2 (Pathway~B) shows evaluation crash with \\emph{increasing} entropy ($-5.21 \\rightarrow -3.83$) while values remain stable. This produces the sole false positive in the NVE classification.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.65\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig6_multiple_pathways.pdf}\n\\caption{Contrasting collapse dynamics. \\textbf{Left:} 008d (CIVO), value explosion with entropy decrease. \\textbf{Right:} 008e\\_v3 s2 (non-CIVO), evaluation crash with entropy increase.}\n\\label{fig:multiple_pathways}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{F7: Full-pathway gradient stopping shows mixed results.}\nExp\\_018 applies Selective Gradient Stopping (SGS) by detaching all non-KL gradients from $e_{\\dt}$; this blocks the observation decoder pathway identified in F2 as the primary CIVO conduit. Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_decomposition} decomposes evaluation returns across conditions. Trained $\\dt$-embedding alone produces $-73\\%$ peak and $-86\\%$ final eval degradation relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 avoids collapse (eval \\sgseval{}, $-51\\%$ final relative to baseline) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8, $-96\\%$); the overall recovery rate is 50\\%. SGS reduces but does not eliminate CIVO risk: the gradient pathway it blocks is necessary but not sufficient for reliable elimination. Reward-only SGS (016e) fails entirely (NVE $10.42\\times$). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (010b) confirms CIVO generalization: evaluation peaks at 321.9 before declining to 51.7 at 105K ($-84\\%$, minimum 22.7 at 65K); peak NVE $8.27\\times$. Full-SGS on Cheetah Run enables recovery from transient CIVO onset (NVE $9.94\\times$ at 45K, eval 4.6) to functional performance (eval 221.5 at 105K), in contrast to permanent collapse without SGS (010b: eval 51.7). Combined with Walker results (50\\% recovery rate), SGS is necessary but not sufficient for CIVO elimination. Removing the embedding on Cheetah eliminates CIVO entirely (024b, 2 seeds, NVE $< 0.2\\times$, eval 96.5/77.3), replicating the Walker causal control (008f) in a second environment.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_civo_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO decomposition: peak and final (105K step) evaluation returns on Walker Walk (DMC) across four conditions. \\textbf{Baseline} (001): vanilla DreamerV3, no temporal embedding. \\textbf{Trained dt-emb} (008d)\\footnotemark: DreamerV3 with a learned $d{=}32$ temporal embedding exposed to multi-rate data. \\textbf{Full-SGS} (s1, s2): trained embedding with Selective Gradient Stopping that detaches all non-KL gradients from $e_{\\dt}$, two seeds. Trained $\\dt$-embedding degrades peak eval by $73\\%$ and final eval by $86\\%$ relative to the baseline. Full-SGS seed~1 partially recovers ($-51\\%$ final, no collapse) but seed~2 collapses ($-96\\%$ final, marked ``collapsed''), indicating that SGS enables recovery from CIVO in some seeds but is not sufficient for reliable prevention (recovery rate $= 50\\%$). All percentages relative to Baseline 001.}\n\\label{fig:civo_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\\footnotetext{Experiment 008d terminated at 55K steps; the 105K data point uses continuation run 009e under identical configuration.}\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% TABLE 2: RSSM Fidelity\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\\dt{=}1$ vs.\\ $\\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}\n\\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{obs\\_MSE}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{reward\\_MAE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\\times$ \\\\\n008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\\times$ \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe trained embedding in 008d produces a $2.78\\times$ reward prediction gap between $\\dt{=}1$ and $\\dt{=}2$ (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}). This systematic bias is internalized by the critic as optimistic value estimates. Having established the mechanistic basis of CIVO, we now ask whether it can be detected during training without access to a reference baseline.\n\n%% ============================================================\n%% F8: Online CIVO Detection\n%% ============================================================\n\n\\subsection{Online CIVO Detection}\n\\label{sec:detector}\n\n\\paragraph{Motivation.}\nThe Normalized Value Excess ($\\nve$) quantifies CIVO severity by comparing the value-to-evaluation ratio against a reference baseline that does not employ temporal abstraction. This comparison is informative for post-hoc diagnosis but unusable during deployment: no practitioner has access to a converged baseline run while training a new agent. A metric that requires a completed control experiment cannot serve as an early warning.\n\nWe therefore ask whether the \\emph{dynamics} of value overestimation, rather than its absolute magnitude relative to an external reference, contain sufficient signal to detect CIVO onset in real time. By tracking the trajectory of the value-to-evaluation ratio $r_t = \\bar{V}_t / \\max(\\bar{E}_t, \\varepsilon)$ (with $\\varepsilon = 10^{-6}$ for numerical stability) across checkpoints, a lightweight rule-based detector can flag dangerous overestimation using only quantities available within a single run.\n\nThe causal structure of CIVO motivates acceleration as the key detection signal. CIVO operates as a positive feedback loop: the trained embedding biases reward predictions, which inflate value estimates, which in turn distort actor gradients toward trajectories that reinforce the bias (\\S\\ref{sec:civo_mechanism}). This self-amplifying cycle produces superlinear growth in $r_t$. In healthy training, a natural negative feedback counteracts value growth: improved value estimates yield a better actor, which raises evaluation returns and stabilizes the ratio. CIVO breaks this negative feedback, so sustained positive acceleration in $r_t$ serves as a causal signature that distinguishes pathological divergence from normal value learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Algorithm.}\nThe detector monitors $r_t$ and fires an alert only when four conditions hold simultaneously at checkpoint $t$ (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:civo_detector}). The conjunction makes the detector conservative: any single noisy checkpoint is unlikely to satisfy magnitude, velocity, acceleration, and decline together. The computational cost is negligible (four scalar comparisons per checkpoint, no additional model inference).\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Online CIVO Detector}\n\\label{alg:civo_detector}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Stream of $(\\bar{V}_t, \\bar{E}_t)$ at each checkpoint $t$\n\\STATE $\\tau_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow 0.49$, \\quad $\\tau_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow 0.1$, \\quad $\\varepsilon \\leftarrow 10^{-6}$\n\\STATE Initialize $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\mathcal{H} \\leftarrow []$\n\\FOR{each checkpoint $t$}\n  \\STATE $r_t \\leftarrow \\bar{V}_t \\,/\\, \\max(\\bar{E}_t,\\, \\varepsilon)$\n  \\STATE $\\Delta r_t \\leftarrow r_t - r_\\text{prev}$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow (r_t \\geq \\tau_\\text{mag})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_t > \\tau_\\text{vel})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{acc} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_\\text{prev} > \\tau_\\text{vel}) \\wedge (\\Delta r_t > \\Delta r_\\text{prev})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{dec} \\leftarrow \\bigl|\\{i : \\mathcal{H}[i] < \\mathcal{H}[i{-}1],\\; i \\in \\text{last 3}\\}\\bigr| \\geq 2$\n  \\IF{$c_\\text{mag} \\wedge c_\\text{vel} \\wedge c_\\text{acc} \\wedge c_\\text{dec}$}\n    \\STATE \\textbf{Alert:} CIVO onset at step $t$\n  \\ENDIF\n  \\STATE $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow r_t$, \\quad $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow \\Delta r_t$\n  \\STATE Append $\\bar{E}_t$ to $\\mathcal{H}$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nThe four conditions capture complementary signals: (1)~\\emph{Magnitude} ($r_t \\geq 0.49$) confirms that the value-to-evaluation ratio has reached a level indicative of overestimation; (2)~\\emph{Velocity} ($\\Delta r_t > 0.1$) rules out slow drift or plateau behavior; (3)~\\emph{Acceleration} ($\\Delta r_t > \\Delta r_{t-1}$, both $> 0.1$) filters oscillatory paths where the ratio rises and falls without sustained divergence; (4)~\\emph{Evaluation decline} ($\\geq 2$ of 3 recent checkpoints decline) confirms that overestimation coincides with actual performance degradation. A 2-of-3 majority threshold balances noise robustness against single-checkpoint evaluation variance with sensitivity to sustained decline.\\footnote{A full comparison of window sizes (3/3, 2/3, 2/5) and their trade-offs appears in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_sensitivity}.}\n\nSensitivity analysis over $\\tau_\\text{mag} \\in [0.30, 0.70]$ confirms that $\\tau_\\text{mag} = 0.49$ maximizes Youden's $J = 0.714$; raising the threshold to $0.50$ loses one true positive (seed~3 triggers at $r_t = 0.497$) with no reduction in false positives, since both false alarms have $r_t > 0.70$ at trigger time. The velocity threshold $\\tau_\\text{vel} = 0.1$ is stable across $[0.05, 0.18]$ (TPR remains $3/3$); we retain $0.1$ to avoid overfitting to $n{=}11$ runs.\n\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\nWe apply the detector retrospectively to eleven runs: trained duration embedding with sustained overestimation (CIVO-positive, $n{=}3$), untrained or absent embedding (CIVO-negative, $n{=}3$), vanilla baselines ($n{=}4$), and one cross-simulator case (PickCube-v1 in ManiSkill3, trained embedding). Ground truth labels derive from manual inspection: a run is CIVO-positive if it exhibits sustained $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ with subsequent evaluation collapse. Table~\\ref{tab:detector-validation} reports all results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Retrospective CIVO detector validation ($n{=}11$). Detection type: ``predictive'' = alert precedes 20\\% eval decline; ``reactive'' = alert follows decline onset.}\n\\label{tab:detector-validation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Exp} & \\textbf{Config} & \\textbf{Verdict} & \\textbf{Trigger} & \\textbf{Type} & \\textbf{Peak Eval} & $r_\\text{ve}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n008d s0 & trained $\\dt$-emb & TP & 45K & predict. & 167 & 0.69 \\\\\n010b & trained $\\dt$-emb (Cheetah) & TP & 65K & reactive & 322 & 3.18 \\\\\n008d s3 & trained $\\dt$-emb & TP & 45K & predict. & 452 & 0.50 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008f s3 & no $\\dt$-emb & TN & -- & -- & 594 & -- \\\\\n009b s2 & trained $\\dt$-emb (self-corr.) & TN & -- & -- & 279 & -- \\\\\nbaseline s1 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 970 & -- \\\\\n009d s0 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 951 & -- \\\\\n009d s1 & vanilla & TN & -- & -- & 920 & -- \\\\\n019b & trained $\\dt$-emb (PickCube) & TN & -- & -- & 26.6 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n009c s2 & no $\\dt$-emb & FP & 105K & reactive & 359 & 0.75 \\\\\n009a s2 & vanilla & FP & 85K & predict. & 967 & 0.73 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{TPR = 3/3 (1.00), \\quad FPR = 2/8 (0.25)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\nThe detector identifies all three CIVO-positive runs (TPR = 3/3). Two of three are detected \\emph{predictively}: 008d seed~0 triggers at 45K, one checkpoint before evaluation crosses the 20\\% decline threshold; seed~3 triggers at 45K, a full 50K steps before decline begins. The third case (010b, Cheetah Run) involves single-step collapse (evaluation drops 80\\% in one interval), and the detector fires reactively at the next available checkpoint. No checkpoint-based monitor can provide advance warning for instantaneous collapse.\n\nTwo false positives occur among eight negative cases (FPR = 2/8, precision = 3/5 = 0.60, F$_1$ = 0.75). Run 009c (no duration embedding) develops genuine late-stage value overestimation unrelated to CIVO; the detector correctly identifies value-performance dissociation, and the alert is arguably useful despite the label mismatch. Run 009a (vanilla baseline) triggers during a transient three-checkpoint eval dip at 85K; the model subsequently recovers to 966, constituting a pure false alarm. In practice, the cost of a false alert (manual checkpoint inspection, minutes) is orders of magnitude lower than the cost of undetected collapse (wasted GPU-hours, corrupted model); moderate FPR is therefore acceptable in this application.\n\nAll three true positives triggered before 65K steps (range 45--65K) while both false positives occurred after 85K (range 85--105K), suggesting that early-training alerts carry higher diagnostic confidence. In practice, alerts before 70K steps can be treated as high-confidence CIVO indicators; later alerts warrant manual inspection.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-simulator specificity (PickCube).}\nExp\\_019b applies the CIVO-trigger configuration (trained $\\dt$-embedding, subsample ON, $H{=}8$) to PickCube-v1 in ManiSkill3, a manipulation task with sparse rewards and qualitatively different dynamics from DMC locomotion. The V/E ratio exhibits an oscillatory pattern rather than monotonic divergence: it spikes to $r_t = 5.66$ at 52.5K (eval crashes from 24.9 to 3.7), then deflates to 1.38 by 72.5K as value estimates self-correct, eventually reaching 0.065$\\times$ NVE at 202.5K. This spike-then-deflation trajectory prevents the acceleration condition from co-occurring with evaluation decline at any single checkpoint. When V/E accelerates during early training (22.5--32.5K, consecutive velocities $+0.50$, $+1.85$), evaluation has not yet declined (C4 fails). When evaluation does decline (42.5--52.5K), the preceding V/E velocity is negative ($-0.81$), so the acceleration condition (C3) fails. Across all 14 checkpoints evaluated through 132.5K, no checkpoint satisfies all four conditions simultaneously, and the detector correctly remains silent (TN). This confirms that the acceleration filter distinguishes transient overestimation spikes from the sustained monotonic divergence characteristic of CIVO. The result extends the detector's specificity validation to a second simulator and a sparse-reward manipulation domain.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nFour constraints bound the current detector. First, the three-checkpoint window cannot distinguish transient dips from sustained collapse (the source of the 009a false alarm). Second, the acceleration condition assumes monotonically increasing overestimation during onset; oscillatory trajectories that eventually collapse could evade detection until acceleration stabilizes. Third, the detector identifies a \\emph{pattern} (rising value-evaluation ratio with declining performance) rather than a \\emph{mechanism}: run 009c shows that non-CIVO pathologies produce the same signature. Fourth, threshold values ($\\tau_\\text{mag} = 0.49$, $\\tau_\\text{vel} = 0.1$) were calibrated on eleven runs across three environments; generalization to different reward scales requires further validation. The detector targets sustained CIVO patterns; transient single-checkpoint spikes (as observed in SGS-protected runs) are by design not flagged, as they do not indicate impending permanent collapse.\n",

Full-SGS seed~1 avoids collapse (eval \\sgseval{}, $-51\\%$ final relative to baseline) while seed~2 collapses (eval 40.8, $-96\\%$); the overall recovery rate is 50\\%. SGS reduces but does not eliminate CIVO risk: the gradient pathway it blocks is necessary but not sufficient for reliable elimination. Reward-only SGS (016e) fails entirely (NVE $10.42\\times$). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run (010b, 2 seeds) confirms CIVO generalization: both seeds collapse (peak NVE $8.27\\times$ and $70.64\\times$, final eval 51.7 and 0.83 at 105K). Full-SGS on Cheetah Run enables recovery from transient CIVO onset (NVE $9.94\\times$ at 45K, eval 4.6) to functional performance (eval 221.5 at 105K), in contrast to permanent collapse without SGS (010b: eval 51.7, 0.83 across 2 seeds). Combined with Walker results (50\\% recovery rate), SGS is necessary but not sufficient for CIVO elimination. Removing the embedding on Cheetah eliminates CIVO entirely (024b, 2 seeds, NVE $< 0.2\\times$, eval 96.5/77.3), replicating the Walker causal control (008f) in a second environment.",

        ]

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Vft4KSfEA1rQNE3dkn1959`</summary>

```text
185	Exp\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \texttt{requires\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.55\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \rightarrow 1.26 \rightarrow 1.45 \rightarrow 0.65 \rightarrow 1.05 \rightarrow 0.60 \rightarrow 0.59 \rightarrow 0.55$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \S\ref{sec:pathways}), indicating that the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.
186	
187	The contrast with exp\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \rightarrow 1.71 \rightarrow 2.09 \rightarrow 4.12$) and triggers full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\times$ before self-correcting to $0.55\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\dt$-embedding must be able to \emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.
188	
189	Together, these two controls establish that CIVO requires the \emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.
```
